# EduSphere — decisions, phase 1 plan, compliance, costs

Approved by Mikey on 2026-09-04 unless marked "proposed".

## Decisions
- Schools remain the core plan. (Considered a pure direct-to-families/home-supplement pivot on 2026-09-05 — weaker core promise since a home tool adds practice time rather than reclaiming school-day time, plus a crowded market with free incumbents like Khan Academy — and decided against pivoting away from schools.)
- Two consent paths, both in scope, sharing the same product: institutional (schools and formally-organized microschools, consenting under COPPA's school exception) and direct-family (homeschool families, via standard parental consent — a parent creates the account, verified the usual way with a payment card). No new build for either; only the signup/consent flow differs.
- Marketing focus is deliberately narrow for now: microschools and homeschool families, because the mastery/reclaim-the-day philosophy is the actual differentiator for them. Marketing to traditional-school families who want supplemental practice on top of normal school is deferred, not rejected — that segment buys on content breadth and price against free incumbents, not philosophy, and the library isn't broad enough yet to win that fight. Self-serve signup isn't blocked to that audience if they find it on their own; the decision is not to spend marketing effort chasing them yet. Revisit once the coverage map is wider and the aligned segments have produced case studies.
- Branching into specialized trades and corporate training stays a goal either way. Already supported structurally: `subject` and `audience` on each course in `logic.mjs` are plain strings, not a K-12-specific enum, exactly so a course can later be "Electrical safety" for "New hires" as easily as "Math" for "Grade 3." No rework needed to add a vertical — just new courses.
- Texas first, so standards alignment is TEKS first; Common Core codes are kept as secondary tags.
- Rostering and sign-in through what schools run (Clever, ClassLink, Google Workspace for Education) are phase 1 requirements, not extras.
- Student identifier = school-issued ID created by an admin or imported from rostering. Students pick from a class list and never type an ID. Admins can edit, rename, deactivate, and merge (a merge is one event that re-tags two histories; nothing is deleted).
- Content: white-glove first (we build early customers' modules), then tiers — Standard (our library + course switches), Plus (AI-assisted upload with admin approval), Enterprise (white-glove onboarding, custom content, SSO, scheduled reports).
- Uploads never design a sequence from scratch: customers place material into a standards-aligned skeleton per grade that is already ordered foundational → advanced; the pedagogy check flags out-of-order material.
- No public ledger (Hedera or other) in the core product. Nothing about a child ever goes on an immutable public chain. Revisit only for anchoring completed credentials, if a customer asks; signed verifiable credentials come first.
- Coverage is proven, not assumed: a coverage map per grade (every standard → modules) with a test that fails while any standard has no module, and a pilot measured against an outside assessment.
- "Wonder" reflection block: open questions with no right answer; typed for readers, pick-a-picture or tell-an-adult for younger children; never graded, never gating; followed by four respectful voices (scientist, artist, grandparent of faith, skeptic) with none declared right, ending on an open question; every prompt and perspective human-approved before publication; must never imply religion or non-belief is wrong.
- Minutes per day is a first-class metric (the efficiency claim). Fun through variety, pictures, and stories — no streak pressure on young children, no dark patterns.
- Design: dark theme as a toggle (default on for educators, light by default for young learners), a modern readable sans (Lexend or Atkinson Hyperlegible), playfulness through color and illustration, no licensed characters. Done after foundations.
- The confidence score is a printed rubric tuned from data, not a copy of any other product's formula.

## Phase 1 plan (needs Mikey's approval before any service is purchased)
- Stack: Python + FastAPI, PostgreSQL, React. Managed Postgres with row-level security so an educator can only read their own students' rows — enforced by the database, not app code.
- Data: `schools`, `classes`, `learners` (ID only), `enrollments`, `courses/modules/questions`, one append-only `events` table (app role: INSERT and SELECT only). Reports are SQL views over `events`; each view's definition is printed on the report; scheduled reports run the same view on a timer and deliver CSV/email.
- Sign-in: class code + pick your ID (+ PIN for older grades); district SSO. No child emails or passwords held by us.
- Backups: the managed database's automated daily backups and point-in-time recovery. No spreadsheets.
- The educator sign-in screen needs a "Forgot password" link once real accounts exist. In the prototype there is only a shared PIN, so there is nothing to recover yet.
- Sign-in fail-safes (raised 2026-09-05, both impossible in the single-device prototype and both real once the app is centralized):
  - Two students signing in as the same person at once: the later sign-in takes over and the earlier session is ended. Both sessions are written to the event log, so an educator can see it happened and when. Whether that also raises an alert is a decision for the pilot, once we know how often it actually occurs.
  - A student picking the wrong name from the list: three protections, in order of cost. The name is shown prominently on the very next screen, so a mistake is visible within seconds. Backing out to the list costs nothing and records no progress against the wrong student. On shared devices, each student gets a short PIN or a picture password, which is how schools already handle this. If work does land on the wrong record, merging is already built and nothing is lost.
- Review scheduling: spaced review across days/weeks using the confidence score (the "reroute by confidence" behavior).
- Voice: device voice now; a paid text-to-speech service is a phase 1 choice (only question text is sent).
- Phase 2 content pipeline (a graph, not a line): Intake → Structurer (modules, order, standards) → checkers: facts (every claim cited), age-appropriateness, coverage (5+ question types, by code) → pedagogy/order check → human lead-admin approval → publish. Any checker can send the draft back with notes. Every step logged. No agent states a fact it cannot cite; practice is computable or sourced. Teacher-suggested edits enter the same flow.

## Compliance rules (every decision is made with these in mind)
- COPPA: schools consent under the school exception; we collect the minimum; parents and schools can request export and deletion; retention is limited to the agreed period.
- FERPA: we act as a school official under a data-processing agreement; data is used only for the school's purposes; never re-shared.
- State laws: Texas student-privacy law and each customer state's equivalent; sign the district's standard data privacy agreement.
- Collected: school-issued ID, class, and events. Never: names, emails, photos, audio, free text from children, ads, trackers. Read-aloud is output only; there is no microphone.
- Before real students: privacy policy, DPA template, parental/school consent flow, review by an education-privacy attorney. Later: a COPPA Safe Harbor program.
- Accessibility: public schools are held to it — readable type, contrast, screen-reader support, captions for any audio.

## When money starts (rough, changeable)
- Phase 0: $0.
- Phase 1 pilot: managed database + small backend host $15–50/month; domain ~$15/year; text-to-speech pennies per thousand characters; content-pipeline AI calls cents to a few dollars per uploaded document.
- Non-software: privacy attorney review (hundreds to a couple thousand dollars); school-required liability insurance; much later a formal security audit (tens of thousands) when a customer requires it.

## Working method (from the Claude Code best-practices guide, adopted)
- CLAUDE.md stays short: commands, rules, gotchas. Detailed how-tos live in `.claude/skills/` and are loaded on demand.
- Every change ends with `./check.sh`; a Stop hook in `.claude/settings.json` runs it automatically (verify the hook with `/hooks` on first use).
- Explore → plan → implement → check for anything larger than a one-sentence diff.
- Larger changes get a fresh-context review by `.claude/agents/reviewer.md`, gaps only.
- `/clear` between unrelated tasks; after two failed corrections, start a clean session with a better prompt.

## Built on 2026-09-04 (free improvements)
- Browser click-through test found and fixed a real bug: the educator's learner list did not refresh during a session.
- Reading course (Kindergarten, Letters): letter names, order, first letters, counting letters, big/small matching — all computable, read aloud, tap only.
- Wonder block: one typed question after fractions mastery, one pick question for kindergarten courses, four voices, closing question; words never stored; counted as reflections on the educator report.

- Transcript: a printable record of every course a student has worked on, kept whether or not the course is still switched on. Courses never opened are left out, because an untouched course says nothing about a student. It carries the school-issued identifier, the modules, the dates and the confidence figures, and nothing a student wrote. That keeps it inside what a school may share under FERPA and what we may hold under COPPA.
- Mastery is defined per module rather than platform-wide. Five questions suits counting to ten; a twelfth grade physics module may want three longer ones. Courses and modules can override both the number asked and the number needed.

- Life skills are suggestions for educators rather than content for students. Nothing is graded, tracked, or shown to a child, which keeps the section outside the student record entirely.
- They are listed as one sequence from earliest to most mature rather than assigned to grades. Grade assignment implies a precision nobody has, and a mixed age classroom has no single grade to look under. The sequence is an order, not a schedule. This also removes most of the reason an administrator would need to edit the list, so editing is not built for now.
- The screen shades each skill from the palest green at the top to deep forest green at the bottom, so the sequence is visible before a word is read. Four stage markers (Early, Growing up, Teen, Nearly grown) break up a long column without splitting the sequence.
- Educators can mark a skill as covered. This belongs to the classroom, never to a student, so no soft judgment about any child enters a record a parent could request. Covered skills are ticked and muted in place rather than moved, with a Hide toggle for when the list gets long. The word is "covered" rather than "complete", because most of these are never really finished.

- Reflection questions require approval by the school before any student sees one. This is the most sensitive content in the platform, so approval is opt in rather than opt out: an unreviewed question simply never appears. An educator reads the prompt, all four voices and the closing question, then approves, removes, or leaves it. Approval can be withdrawn at any time. This is also a selling point worth stating plainly to schools, since no reflection content reaches a child that somebody there has not read.
- There is nothing for an educator to review in a student's own reflection answers, because those are never saved.

- Onboarding asks for a rough starting level (Early years, Elementary, Middle school, High school and beyond) so the placement check begins near the child and an older student never sees pre-K questions. It is a starting point the check adjusts, not a label.
- Only the early years are asked for a sign-in picture, at the moment of adding, and it can be added or changed later from the roster. Pictures are drawn in the app from a fixed set of eight; photo upload is deliberately not offered.
- Courses containing a module that needs a touch screen carry a warning where the educator assigns them.
- For pre-reader courses, finished modules fold behind one star row so the next thing to do is seen first.

- Sign-in pictures are twelve drawn animals on six background colours, seventy-two in all, so a large school never needs two children to share one. Adding a young student suggests an unused picture automatically, and a duplicate is flagged rather than forbidden.
- Operations are written down in docs/RUNBOOK.md: managed services for everything in phase 1, a developer on retainer, a status page, and a downtime procedure that starts with rollback.

- Development in phase 1 is done through Claude Code against this repository, with `./check.sh` as the gate, rather than a developer on retainer. One human (Mikey) holds the production credentials and the three buttons: roll back, restore a backup, post to the status page. A freelancer's name is kept on file for hands-on help by the half day.
- Phase 1 hosting: Supabase for the database and sign-in (managed Postgres, automatic backups, point-in-time restore, a readable dashboard, a signable data processing agreement) and Vercel for the app (deploy from the repository, one-click rollback). Chosen for status pages and rollback buttons a non-technical owner can use.

- Local-first, decided 2026-09-10 in answer to Mikey's question. The record of what happened is the whole truth, so a file holding every record is a complete backup, and restoring it recreates the classroom on any device. This removes the database, the accounts, the hosting bill and, most importantly, any child's record on a server we would have to protect. The cost is a habit: someone presses Download backup, and the app nags after seven days. Restoring merges by student id and joins event logs, so it can only ever add history. A server is deferred until a customer genuinely needs cross-device sync, which is a large-school problem, not a microschool one. Automatic emailing of reports is not possible without a server; a downloaded file the educator emails to themselves is the equivalent.

- Backup cannot email itself without a server. On a phone or iPad the Share button opens the device's own sheet, where Mail is one tap away; elsewhere it downloads. File names carry the device name, the student count and the date (edusphere-ipad-3-12-students-2026-09-10.json) so a folder from ten devices reads at a glance. A touchless auto-save to a chosen file is possible in Chrome and on Chromebooks and is a next step; iPads do not support it.
- Educators stay signed in while active. After twenty quiet seconds a warning appears; after thirty the PIN is asked again (2026-09-11, Mikey's call: five minutes was long enough for a student to reach the settings on a shared device). Any tap or key resets the clock. Returning within five minutes of the last activity skips the PIN.

- Loop-back routing, built 2026-09-10: two failed rounds in a row on a module reopen its nearest prerequisite for a quick check, even one mastered before. The result screen says so in plain words and offers it as the one thing to do. It is recorded as an event so the educator's module story mentions it. The prerequisite graph is what makes this reach across grades once content exists there.
- An educator creates an account once, from a Create account link under Educator Login: they choose a PIN (four to six digits, a convenience lock stored scrambled on the device), name the device, and pick their state, with an i tip explaining why the state matters. After that, Educator Login asks for the PIN and nothing else. The prototype PIN 2468 still opens the door on a device with no account, so testers are never locked out.
- The Classroom page no longer shows the coverage line; the state and a Change state link sit under the i tip beside the roster text.

- Content written 2026-09-10 while Mikey was away: kindergarten math gained one more/one less, joining and taking away (story word problems), comparing numbers, and shapes; kindergarten reading gained letter sounds and rhymes. Every new generator is re-derived independently in the tests from the question's own words, across 300 seeds. Kindergarten math now covers 13 of 18 TEKS standards, kindergarten reading 5 of 10.
- Word lists for reading (SOUND_WORDS, RHYME_FAMILIES) use only words a kindergartener says and hears. Adding to them is how reading content grows; the tests check every combination automatically.

- All states, decided 2026-09-10: a module is the unit and a standard is a label, so every standard in the plan carries a framework (TEKS or CCSS) and every course is mapped to both. An educator chooses their state once at first sign-in; the Classroom page shows coverage against that state's framework. Fifty states plus DC are listed. Adding a framework means adding its codes to the standards, not new content.
- Updates reach educators by opening the site, because the app is served from a static address and holds no accounts. A backup fixture per format version is kept in tests/fixtures and must restore forever.
- Onboarding documents for whoever comes next: CLAUDE.md now opens with a reading order; docs/VISION.md holds the why; docs/ALGORITHMS.md explains every number and marks which are judgements.

- The class view ("Who needs help"), built 2026-09-10: every active student ranked by a need score built only from what is in their record (modules stuck on, loop backs, guessing flags, low confidence on mastered work, time since last practice), with the reasons listed in words so the score is never a mystery, three bands (needs help now, keep an eye on, on track), a one-sentence summary at the top, and Open report on every row.
- The spoken Wonder for pre-readers: one spoken question, large tap targets, two short voices shown one at a time and spoken as they appear, offered once after a mastery and only when approved.

- Letter tracing, built 2026-09-10, is the first touch-screen module and the answer to "how do they learn to write without paper". Twelve capital letters are defined as strokes on a 100 by 100 grid; the pad shows them faintly with a green starting dot and an arrow; the finger's path is checked by arithmetic: every stroke must be visited in order within a tolerance that forgives a wobbly hand, and most of the drawing must lie on the letter so a scribble fails. The Letters course now carries the touch-screen warning. Lowercase letters and curves beyond O and C are the next step.

- Reflection answers stay unsaved, decided again 2026-09-10 after the move to local-first. The legal exposure shrank, but two reasons stand: a child who knows a teacher will read it writes to perform rather than to think, and anything stored travels in backup files that get emailed around. If Mikey wants it, the honest form is an educator switch, off by default, with answers excluded from backups unless explicitly included.
- "Arithmetic, not AI" means: built from checked rules and word lists, never composed by a language model at question time. Open writing is the stated exception: prompts from templates, structure checked by rule, quality judged by a person or an AI assistant with a teacher confirming.
- Browser account sync does not share EduSphere data: browsers sync settings, not a site's storage. One device per student, or restore before switching. Subscription tiers by device count are fine; sync is not to be promised.
- Selling and sign-in without a server of ours: a static landing page with Buy and Log in, purchase through a payment service that issues a license key at checkout, the key checked offline by the app. The only thing maintained is the payment dashboard. The page also lists permissions (allow downloads for backups; no microphone; no account) and device advice (Chromebook or desktop Chrome for touchless backup, iPad for finger tracing).
- Pre-K courses are non-linear: parallel activities where only named prerequisites apply. Pre-K standards come from the Texas Prekindergarten Guidelines and the federal Head Start Early Learning Outcomes Framework.

- Reflection answers: confirmed unsaved, 2026-09-10.
- A vendor-held copy of every backup: declined 2026-09-10. It would make EduSphere the custodian of every child's record from every school, which is the server-side data the local-first design avoids, and it needs a data agreement under FERPA and COPPA. Support instead runs on the school's own cloud folder (touchless backup into Drive) and files shared on request. A per-school opt-in under a data agreement is a phase 1 possibility, after the attorney review.

- The product ships as `index.html`, built by `tools/build-site.mjs` with React bundled in and storage on the browser's localStorage, so GitHub Pages or any plain host can serve it and nothing is fetched at run time. The `.jsx` artifact remains for the chat preview only. A test reloads the standalone page in a real browser and checks a student survives the reload.

- Desktop layout: the content column widens to 720px on screens over 1000px, the welcome card sits lower, and on screens over 1240px a pair of quiet arcs in the logo's green fill the edges without ever touching a tap target. The backup link glows in place when a backup is due, rather than carrying a halo.

- A forgotten PIN is reset only with a backup file of this classroom, decided 2026-09-11 after Mikey pointed out that a student on a shared device could otherwise reset the PIN and tamper with records. Every backup carries a recovery code made when the account was created; the reset checks it. A student does not have the educator's backup file. Each reset is recorded in the profile and shown on the Classroom page. The PIN itself is never in a backup or a file name. Two limits stated plainly: a brand-new account with no backup cannot be reset (the Classroom page nudges for a first backup), and a student who knows browser developer tools can read anything stored locally, which is the floor for any offline app and why the PIN is called a convenience lock.
- Sign out on the Classroom page ends the educator session at once, so the next person at a shared device meets the PIN screen.
- Contact us opens a small popup with the address and an email button, rather than showing the address beside the link.

- The recovery code sits first in every backup file with a note saying what it is for, shows on the Backup page to a signed-in educator, and can be typed on the Forgot PIN screen instead of choosing the file. Nobody has to open a backup to find it, but anyone who does sees it at once.

- Reflections come round after every two mastered modules (Mikey asked for every two or three, 2026-09-11), drawn from a pool shared across courses at the same stage and rotated so the least recently answered comes first. Six more questions were written so the rotation has something to rotate through: three for the early years, three for grades 3 to 5. The logo is now the browser-tab icon, built from the same drawing.

- A reflection question is never offered a third time to the same student (WONDER_MAX_REPEATS = 2), decided 2026-09-11. Once every approved question in a stage has been answered twice, the reflection is skipped rather than repeated. Pool size therefore governs how often reflections appear, never whether one repeats. The pool grew to 36 (17 early years, 19 grades 3 to 5) and should keep growing; the teen and grown stages have none yet because no courses exist there.

- Reflection themes, decided 2026-09-11 from Mikey's list: world (curiosity), failure (failure is how learning happens), feelings (naming and handling them), ups-and-downs (hard days are normal and we get through them, without getting tied up in our own narratives). Every question carries one. The cadence counts finished rounds, pass or fail, not masteries, so a struggling child meets reflections more often; after a failed round the failure, feelings and ups-and-downs questions come first. The pool is 56 and a coverage check notes any stage with fewer questions than modules, so the pool keeps pace as the curriculum grows.

- Three.js is not used, decided 2026-09-11 when Mikey offered it. It weighs about 600 KB and needs a graphics context that some locked-down school devices disable, so it would slow the first load and fail quietly on the machines a microschool is likely to have. The 3D look is achieved with gradients in the SVG drawings we already use (shaded solids and counting balls), which weigh nothing and render everywhere. Three.js stays an option for one specific future screen, such as a solid a child can spin, loaded only there.

- Young learners, redefined 2026-09-11 on Mikey's instruction: a student with ANY early-years course (pre-K to grade 2) still unfinished gets the handheld screens, whatever else is assigned. Early courses list first in every subject, the prerequisite graph keeps advanced work locked until the early work it depends on is mastered, and the star row counts only early modules. Once every early course is complete the student graduates to the regular screens.
- A new student starts on the recommended courses for their level (the two lowest grades of their band), not on every course. Course lists on the report run pre-K first, college last.
- Every user-facing string is checked for American spelling and house style by tests/spelling.test.mjs (step 3 of check.sh). Contractions are welcome. Mikey's exact wording is never overridden once given.
- The educator report: a collapsible bulleted summary (open by default), a collapsible Assigned now list (closed), all per-course progress behind one Progress by course dropdown with an i tip (closed), and Key Words - Explained with each term folded until tapped. Reviewed Wonder questions fold under a Reviewed row per stage so the next awaiting question is always at the top. Every i tip shares one gradient look and closes on sign out and on leaving a screen.

- index.html stays one generated file on purpose: it runs from GitHub Pages, a USB stick or a double-click with nothing to fetch, and its length costs nothing because nobody edits it. The sources are already split (src/logic.mjs, src/curriculum.mjs, src/ui.jsx) and those are what get edited.
- New students start on the lowest grade of their band only (pre-K for early years). Finishing every module of a course switches on the next course up in that subject automatically, recorded as a courses_enabled event so the educator sees it and can change it. A placement check, once built, will jump further.
- Young learners' subject cards breathe (a gentle scale and soft shadow on the card itself) instead of carrying a halo that grew beyond the screen; locked modules are not shown to them; the finished-modules fold sits below the available modules. Reflection screens open with a sliding "Let's Wonder!" title. The matching lesson shows two shapes side by side.
- The test browser runs with reduced motion, because a card that breathes forever never looks still to an automated click.

- The zip is the repository: it unpacks without a wrapper folder and contains exactly what GitHub should hold (no dist/). A .gitignore keeps .DS_Store and test output out. The tab icon is a bold drawing of its own (green disc, gold arc, white E) because the full logo's arcs vanish at sixteen pixels. On laptops the welcome page is a full-height column: logo a third bigger, a taller sign-in box, the three links at the foot and larger, and the side arcs wider; the pointer fades letters as well as arcs.

- Wide screens (2026-09-11): the content column zooms by 8 percent so text and taps grow together, sections get more air, the Classroom intro is padded to wrap in two lines with the i kept on the line, Wonder Questions and Life Skills sit side by side, the Active Students dropdown and its list share one box, Add someone new and Who needs help narrow to 380px, and the backup link is larger. Phones are unchanged.
- A pre-K reading course (rhymes, first sounds, big letters; readAloud; non-linear) so early learners start with both subjects, mapped to the Texas Prekindergarten Guidelines and Head Start; with its own reflection.
- GitHub: publish the unzipped folder itself as the repository through GitHub Desktop (Add Local Repository, create a repository here, Publish); updates are drag-over-replace then Commit and Push.

- Young learners see only their early-years courses on the overview (2026-09-11): anything above grade 2, even if assigned, waits until every early course is complete. Their overview is a full-height column with the greeting centered and larger, no course labels between modules, a star row at the foot that fills from the left like counting, and the practice-again fold beneath the stars. Tapping an answer plays a soft two-note blip made by the browser itself; moving on is silent. The voice prefers warm female voices and speaks a little brighter.
- Welcome page: Educator Login is a green button, the sign-in card is calmer, and the name grid scales with the number of students. The logo's pointer fade is tighter so smaller pieces vanish, and the side arcs draw themselves in once.

- A student record with no course decision gets the starter set for the student's level the first time it is opened (withStarterCourses), so students added before the starter rule are not handed every course. The privacy note sits at the very foot of the welcome page; the PIN screen sits lower on large screens; Who needs help matches Add someone new with its explanation under an i; the Key Words card is darker.

- Spoken lesson lines read like a person talking (2026-09-11): every line must contain a real sentence of four or more words unless it only names letters or counts aloud, and every picture must match the words spoken over it (a shown word is said, a shape is named, a highlight appears in every word shown). Instruction lines are exempt from the word match because a reading lesson must show a word without saying it. tests/scripts.test.mjs enforces both and is step 4 of check.sh. Thirty-seven lines were reworded, such as "Cat. Hat. These two words have endings that sound alike."
- Letter pictures can carry a highlight ('at' for a rhyme ending, 'first' for an opening sound); those letters turn gold and grow while the rest stay quiet.
- Better voices, phase 1 plan: record every fixed spoken line once with a high-quality service at build time and ship the audio with the site, keeping the device voice as the fallback and for prompts whose numbers change. Not built now because it needs a paid key and a network.

- Young learners have no practice-again list (2026-09-11, Mikey's call). Mastered modules return by themselves as memory checks inside later rounds, a child that age does not self-direct review, and a teacher who wants a module redone resets it from the report. The star row stays as the only thing at the foot of their overview.

- Halos are gone everywhere (2026-09-11, Mikey's call): an inviting button pulses itself gently (edu-pulse, a five percent scale) instead of casting a ring around it. The Let's Wonder! title bounces in and shimmers between green and gold. The voice is a little slower (rate 0.86). Two faint spheres drawn from the logo's own arcs flank it on large screens. Wonder Questions and Life Skills cards are green and light gold with their buttons aligned at the foot.

- Cross-course prerequisites gate a module only when the earlier course is assigned to the student (2026-09-11, after Mikey found an Elementary student locked out of every grade 3 reading module). Placement is the educator's judgment: leaving grade 2 off means the student is past it, so grade 3 opens. In-course chains always hold, and loop-back still uses the full prerequisite list, so a struggling student is sent back to the earlier module regardless. gatingPrerequisites and moduleStatuses(progress, enabledIds) carry this; the report, class view and student summary use it.

- While an educator is signed in, a small chip at the top right of every educator screen counts down to sign-out ("Logging out in 30 seconds"); mouse movement, wheel, scrolling, typing and clicking reset it. Touchless backup reports what it last did on the Classroom page and the Backup page, and a failed write switches it off with a visible note instead of silently. The Hear it again control is a speaker button in the top-right corner of the card, and the "on silent" note sits at the foot of the page once anything has been spoken.

- After an idle sign-out, unlocking with the PIN returns the educator to the page and scroll position they were on; Change state does the same. A completed course on the transcript names the standards it satisfied in the state's framework (Common Core until a state is chosen), read from the curriculum plan; a coverage check requires every course to map to at least one code in each framework.

- Touchless backup writes into a chosen folder, one file per day named by the date and rewritten through the day (2026-09-12, answering Mikey's question about a file per module). A backup holds the whole classroom, so a file per completed round would run to thousands of files and gigabytes; daily snapshots give history at a few megabytes a year for a large class. Where a browser offers a file picker but no folder picker, the older single rewritten file is used.

- Touchless backup remembers its folder across browser restarts (the folder handle is kept in the browser's own store, 2026-09-12, after Mikey pointed out that a folder forgotten at every restart made the feature a false promise). After a restart the Classroom page checks: where the browser has kept its permission the daily files resume by themselves; otherwise a single Resume touchless backup tap grants it again. The wording everywhere now says exactly that. A truly unattended backup with no tap on any device would need a server, which is deferred.

- Grade 4 reading (summarizing, making inferences, similes and metaphors, how a text is built; six built passages with summaries, inferences and clues) and grade 5 math (adding and subtracting decimals, multiplying fractions, dividing by two-digit numbers, volume, order of operations) added 2026-09-12 in the same shapes as every other course: five generators per module, every math generator re-derived independently, standards mapped to TEKS and Common Core and marked ready, a reflection per course. 74 modules, 76 reflections. - 2026-09-13: teaching text is rendered through a small markup (newline per line, **bold**, [[centered equation]]) and written in the drawn-out, repeated, Gladwell-for-a-younger-reader style Mikey specified (see DESIGN.md). Four lessons rewritten as models; equation explanations walk one step per line; question screens put the question first and center everything; a wrong answer among named kinds (appeals, flaws, ironies) gets a sentence about the chosen kind; a round never repeats a question (buildAttempt re-rolls duplicates). The appeals questions had generic job pairs that did not match some statements; each statement now carries its own pair. Share is off everywhere (failed on phone and laptop). A hidden student can be deleted for good from the Inactive list, after confirmation. My progress counts the student's own modules, not every module in the platform. docs/social-preview.png (1280 x 640) is the GitHub social preview. Touch-only modules: the tracing pad accepts a mouse or trackpad, a note says a touch screen is easier, and a round left by the X records nothing; a phone QR hand-off was declined because the phone would not hold the student's record.

- 2026-09-13, second pass: a formatting pass (formatTeachingText) opens up every lesson, key idea and explanation that is still in the old dense form (one sentence per line, equations in bold, a sentence that is mostly an equation centered, x emphasized), so the rules apply everywhere at once; 52 lessons are now hand-written in the drawn-out style (all of grades 4 to 10 math and grades 4 to 9 reading, plus the main idea); the remaining read-aloud and early lessons keep their spoken scripts and are opened up by the pass. - Mastery v2 (decided 2026-09-13, to build in phase 1; Mikey's point that one short explanation and 4 of 5 on a single round is thin). A module is mastered after two passing rounds on different days: the first pass unlocks the next module so nobody waits, the second earns the star. Two memory checks per round, drawn from any mastered module across courses. A cumulative checkpoint round of eight questions after every fourth module, no new material. Two or three worked examples per lesson at rising difficulty. This changes what an attempt_completed event means for mastery, so it needs its own migration rule (existing single-pass masteries stay mastered) and a pass through every test that masters a module with one perfect set.

Every grade from pre-K through 12 now has math, and every grade from 1 through 11 has reading. 137 modules, 106 reflections. NEXT: grade 12 reading, then the college band, then mastery v2.

- Mastery v2 refinement (2026-09-13, after Mikey asked about three rounds): two spaced passes for the star, with the third retrieval coming from a memory check inside another module or a cumulative checkpoint rather than a third full round, because spacing holds better than volume and a third grind risks turning the platform into a chore. A missed checkpoint question lowers confidence and queues a refresher round; it never removes the star.

- Pre-K 3 added 2026-09-13: Very first steps (red and blue, find the match, one and two, more) and Listen and point (circle and square, A and B, which one is different), all spoken, two choices per question, mapped to the Texas Prekindergarten Guidelines and the Head Start ELOF. Both courses start hidden: a new early-years student still begins at pre-K 4, and an educator switches pre-K 3 on for a three-year-old. Every grade from pre-K 3 through college now has a course in both subjects. 157 modules, 112 reflections. NEXT: Mastery v2, then a third subject.

- Mastery v2 shipped 2026-09-14 (core): deriveProgress counts passes and the distinct local days they fell on; a pass (passed) unlocks the next module and the next course and joins the memory-check pool; the star (mastered) needs passes on two different days; passes dated before 2026-09-14, or with an unreadable time, keep the star on their own so nobody loses one. Status 'passed' appears on the overview (hollow star, Passed once, Pass it again), in My progress (its own group), in the report rows and module story, on the transcript, in the who-needs-help why-sentence, and on the result screen ("Come back on another day and pass it again to earn the star"). Still to build from the same design: a second memory check per round, and the cumulative eight-question checkpoint after every fourth module with a refresher rather than a lost star.

- Two memory checks per round shipped 2026-09-14: the second comes from a different passed module than the first, from anywhere the student has passed; with only one passed module there is one check, never a repeat. Both count in the review tally, in confidence, and are named on the result screen ("Memory checks from earlier: count to 5 correct, letter names missed"). The event carries review2 beside review, so old records read unchanged. Still to build: the cumulative eight-question checkpoint after every fourth module.

- Cumulative checkpoint shipped 2026-09-14: due when the number of passed modules reaches a multiple of four and no checkpoint has been done at that count; eight questions from passed modules, never a repeat, offered on the overview to readers (young learners keep memory checks only); recorded as a checkpoint_completed event with per-module results; a miss counts in confidence like a missed memory check (a weak return now costs a point) and marks the module for a refresher on the overview, the report and the module story, cleared by passing the module again; the checkpoint result screen names the modules worth a refresher and says nothing passed is lost. Mastery v2 is now complete: spaced two-pass mastery, two cross-course memory checks per round, and checkpoints.

STANDING RULE: every pass adds to pre-K 3 through grade 2. This pass: Trace a big letter (L, T, O) in pre-K 4 reading and Writing numbers in grade 1 math, both stylus. 300 modules.

- 2026-09-14: every writing lesson, all 32 across grades 2 to 12, was drawn out much further at Mikey's request: one idea per line, each example labeled with its part in bold (reason, evidence, claim, warrant, beginning, middle, end), a worked example in every lesson, and the argument-with-evidence lesson in his words (an argument is essentially an opinion essay with evidence; reasons need facts to support them, examples to demonstrate them and quotations to show what an expert or a witness said; a reason without evidence is merely a feeling). STANDING RULE, restated: lessons are drawn out to this degree, not summarized.

- 2026-09-14: automatic backups now fire when a round finishes a whole course rather than after every round (per module was too many files); all text says per course.

- 2026-09-14, from Mikey's phone: the student cards on the Classroom page now put the picture, name and band centered across the top (with the starting grade when one was chosen) and Open report at the bottom right beside the action links, so long names no longer wrap beside the button. The logout chip shows only for the last ten seconds of the minute rather than counting down the whole time.

- 2026-09-14: the logout chip counts down the whole minute after all (Mikey: it reminds an educator not to hand the device to a student too soon); on phones it sits centered at the foot, on laptops bottom right.

- 2026-09-14: after a full progress reset the placement check comes back: the gate counted practice rounds from before the reset as reasons not to offer it (the placed events were already scoped by activeEvents; the attempts were not). Fixed by scoping the attempt check to events after the last reset.

- (2026-09-14) The placement check covers Math, Reading and Science only, on purpose. Writing is marked by the educator, so nothing in it can be scored by the app. History is territories, not rungs (Texas in grade 4, world history in grade 10): clearing grade 10 says nothing about grade 4, and the walk down would land a tenth grader in Texas history. When history needs a skip, it will be per module (a short check that places that one module), never the grade ladder. Until then the report's "Unlock it anyway" covers a transfer student.
- (2026-09-14) Grade 12 social studies is two courses under the History subject, United States government (government-12) and Economics and personal finance (economics-12), because Texas teaches them as two half-year courses and the transcript's standards lines stay clean. Every economics answer is computed and re-derived in the tests; every dated government fact carries a story.
- (2026-09-14) Every dated history question explains a wrong answer with the fact and a short story behind it (HISTORY_WHY in logic.mjs, `why` on the government and amendment lists). Mikey's rule: a bare date is unhelpful, a story sticks. The generator sweep fails if any dated question lacks one.
- (2026-09-14) Style checks tightened at Mikey's request: key ideas may not run a sentence past 28 words, and every lesson sentence starts with a capital and ends with a mark (tests/spelling.test.mjs). Student names are capped at 30 characters on add and rename; names already on a roster are never cut.

- (2026-09-14) The class view names a single missed round ("missed the last round of the thirteen colonies (2 of 5 right); a second miss in a row would route back to the fundamentals") and, when nothing is mastered yet, how many rounds there have been. Mikey tested a deliberate fail and the row said "Nothing to flag", which was false. The report summary now ends with practice so far: rounds, passes, and anything tried but not passed with its best score.
- (2026-09-14) The Standards map is grouped by grade (or by subject, one tap) with light-green dropdowns and white bodies; the reading lists left it, since they have their own page. The printed map keeps the same grouping and shows everything open.

- (2026-09-14) Tracing lessons demonstrate instead of describing. Mikey saw "Start at dot number 1. Draw a line down." over a picture of one dot and said it made no sense to a three-year-old. Every tracing lesson (pre-K marks and strokes, connect the dots, letters, numbers, shapes) now shows the stroke drawing itself, slowly, on a loop, with the same start dot and arrow the practice pad uses, and the words say "Watch the line" first.

- (2026-09-14) One practice line, `practiceLine(report)`, feeds both the report summary and every class-view row ("Practice so far: 3 rounds, 1 passed. Tried but not passed yet: ..."), so the two screens can never disagree. On the report each tried module's name is a button that opens its story. On the Standards map a ready grade with gaps shows its count in clay.

- (2026-09-14) The quick check, "I already know this": the next available module in any subject except Writing and the spoken courses offers five questions, one per generator slot, no lesson, scored at the module's own pass mark. A pass emits the same `placed` event placement uses (passed for gating, never mastered, star still to earn, memory checks keep visiting), marked `quickCheck: true` so it never counts as the subject's placement. A miss records the try, opens the lesson, and never feeds the loop back. Three answers under two seconds mean nobody read the questions, and the check does not place. One try per module. Educators can switch it off for the classroom. Mikey chose every subject, the round's own pass mark, and the switch.
- (2026-09-14) History now runs every grade from 4 to 12: grade 6 world cultures, grade 7 Texas history and grade 9 world geography joined, with every number computed and re-derived and every dated fact carrying its story. Codes for these three cite the TEKS by strand or era rather than by knowledge-statement number, which I could not verify offline.

- (2026-09-14) History now runs from kindergarten to grade 12. Kindergarten to grade 2 community and civics are spoken courses (civics-k Me and my community, civics-1 Our community, civics-2 Our community's story), three modules each; grade 3 Communities and government is a reading course. Texas TEKS cited by strand. Short word choices, as the spoken science courses already use.

The sweep fails if a mapped generator ever loses its story. Fifty more one-line science generators remain; add them to the map as they come up.

- (2026-09-15) SCIENCE_WHY second batch: thirty-five more fact generators carry a story after the answer (forces, life-cycle stages, weather tools, food-web roles, cell parts, weathering, adaptations, circuits, the Earth's motions, the water cycle, organs and systems, pressure and fronts, atoms, motion graphs, genes, photosynthesis and respiration, evidence for evolution, homeostasis, the periodic table, bonds, energy, circuits, the moon, base pairs). The three generators whose answers are long example sentences (energy forms, heat transfer, reaction types) already explain themselves and stay as they are.

Student card: on a laptop the name, grade and links stack on the left with Open report on the right, centered on them; on a phone the button sits below the links, centered. Notes: new empty-state words, examples in the placeholder, and a rename from a student ID to a nickname writes "Student ID: <id>" as the first note. Half the pictures are filled by tapping a part and half are drawn on freely with a finger (`COLORING_MODE`), so both kinds of hand are practised. The fold wears the crayons: the whole strip drifts through them with two sparkles crossing corner to corner.
- (2026-09-16) The Slower and Faster buttons on tracing lessons are gone at Mikey's request; each lesson keeps its own pace (pre-K slow, grade 1 quicker).
- (2026-09-16) The coloring fold stays rather than becoming an automatic pop-up: a student choosing which picture to color is part of the reward, and the break already ends itself. If it should also appear on its own after a module is mastered, that hooks the same screen from the results page.

- (2026-09-16) Connect the dots must be joined in order: `traceMatches` now checks that a dots picture reaches dot 1 before dot 2 and so on. Covering the same points out of sequence used to pass.
- (2026-09-16) Let's Color, rebuilt: twenty pictures ordered simplest first (a two-part ball for a three-year-old, a twenty-part city by the end of grade 2) plus a page that outlines the student's own name to draw over. One more picture is earned for every module passed, not every course. The fold is wordless: a grid of black-and-white drawings, locked ones behind a padlock, available ones breathing, and a picture that has had its five minutes rests for fifteen while its square fills with color. The five minutes and the rest are kept on the device (never in the record) and pick up where they stopped, so leaving and coming back does not hand back a fresh five minutes. The buttons are stacked, the canvas fits the screen, a picture opens at the top of the page, and a tap-to-fill picture still lets the page scroll.
- (2026-09-16) A closed subject keeps breathing while another fold is open; it used to stop as soon as anything else was opened.

Every pre-K module carries a `skill`, and `SKILL_ORDER` sets the order. This is a display grouping on the student's overview only — courses, subjects, standards, transcripts and reports are untouched, which is why it needed no changes there. From kindergarten up the screen goes back to subjects.
- (2026-09-16) A star now earns a coloring break without hunting for it: the results screen sends the student to the first picture that is ready, and the break ends itself. A break is noted on the student's log (`colored`) so an educator can see where the time went; the report says how many breaks were taken, and it is never a score, never progress and never on the transcript. Four more intricate pictures (garden, playground, farm, birthday) bring the set to twenty-four.

Pictures can now use curves (`{ t: 'path' }`), and tools/… a one-page sheet of every picture is rendered from the source when judging the art.
- (2026-09-16) A young learner never sees a module that is waiting for another day: passing it once takes it off the screen until tomorrow, when it returns saying "Passed once. Pass again to master." A skill or subject with nothing left to do today drops off the screen entirely. `waitingForAnotherDay` in logic.mjs decides it, so the rule is tested rather than guessed.

- (2026-09-16) The grade line under a name on the login screen shows at every width, not only on a phone. The card behind the coloring pictures wears the crayon wash, with the picture squares white so the black-and-white drawings still read against it; a locked square is translucent instead.

A CSS animation starts with its element, so a fold that was opened and closed came back out of step with the others; every breathing card now carries a negative animation delay measured from when the page opened, which drops it straight back into the same rhythm.

- (2026-09-16) The name page lays a name out instead of stretching it: words (and hyphenated parts) are packed into up to three lines of about twelve characters, and the letters are sized to fit the square, so "Michael-Rafael-Angelo" reads on three lines and "Jo" fills the page. It is never squeezed sideways.
- (2026-09-16) A drawing picture has three nibs: thin, medium and thick, shown as dots of that very size in the color chosen, with thin as the default. The old single line was too fat for a name page.

- (2026-09-16) Breathing cards share one beat for good: the animation never stops now, and a card that is open simply turns it down with a `--beat` variable inside the keyframes. Stopping and restarting was what put a reopened card on its own clock; measured with `document.getAnimations()`, every card now reads the same phase before and after a fold is opened.

- (2026-09-16) Fifteen crayons, five to a row. A resting picture goes grey behind a ring that empties over the fifteen minutes, rather than filling slowly with color, so it is plain at a glance that it is having a break.

Four steps, always about the middle, so no arrows are needed; the drawing keeps its own coordinates, so color put on close up sits exactly where it belongs when they zoom back out. A fourth, finer nib was added for a stylus, and the grid thumbnails now sit square in their tiles.

- (2026-09-16) Zoom stops at 2.2, not 3.6: the deepest step filled the square with one region and lost the picture. The crayons are a little smaller on a phone so all three rows fit under the picture, and keep their old size from tablet width up.

- (2026-09-16) A resting picture is covered in flat grey that sweeps away like a clock hand as the fifteen minutes run down, so the drawing comes back into view rather than a ring counting at it. No green, no dimming of the picture itself.

- (2026-09-16) Zoomed in, a small arrow appears on each edge that still has picture beyond it, and on no other edge, so a student can reach the corners without a permanent set of controls cluttering the square. Zooming keeps the middle of what they were looking at. The colorable background now extends well past the picture, so a filled background covers the square at any zoom.

- (2026-09-16) A star no longer drops a student into a coloring picture. It pulled them out of a run of work, it always opened the same first picture, and it handed back whatever they had already colored on it. Pictures wait in Let's Color, where the student chooses one.

- (2026-09-16) Every nib is finer: the old second-thickest is now the thickest and the thinnest is finer than anything offered before. On a laptop the four nibs sit beside the crayons, two at each side, finest top-left and thickest bottom-right, so nothing needs scrolling; on a phone they stay in their row beneath the colors.

- (2026-09-16) On a drawing picture the outline is laid over the ink, so coloring never buries the lines a student is trying to stay inside. The name page does the same with its letters.
- (2026-09-16) On a wide screen the picture, the countdown bar and the crayons share one width in the same grid column, and the nibs stand clear to their left, ending level with the top of the first row of colors. A drawing picture's shapes are filled without their own outline and the outline is drawn once over the ink, so the lines stay single width rather than doubling.
- (2026-09-16) A wider screen gets six more crayons, twenty-one in three rows running the width of the picture, and the nibs stand in a column beside the picture with two more thicknesses, six in all. A phone keeps fifteen crayons and four nibs in the arrangement Mikey picked. The extras are hidden by class rather than by counting, so both layouts come from the same markup.

- (2026-09-16) The pictures were redrawn with curves instead of stacked shapes, and given scenes rather than single objects: the tree has a canopy, a trunk that flares at the ground, four apples, a cloud, a bird and a sun; the fish has fins, a smile, bubbles, weed and sand; the house has a chimney with smoke, a door knob and a path; the balloon has a second balloon and a curling string; the flower has real petals and leaves; the garden has three flowering plants, a cloud and a sun. More to color, and it looks like the thing it is.

- (2026-09-16) Every remaining picture was redrawn the same way: curved silhouettes and a small scene around the subject (a car on a road under a cloud, a train with smoke, a castle with flags and battlements, a farm with a silo and a sheep, a city under a moon and stars). A part can now be a line rather than a filled region (`line: true`), which is what a balloon string, an antenna or a swing rope needs; filling them left white blobs where the color should have flowed past. From here, new pictures follow the same rule: curves, a few extra things to color, and lines drawn as lines.

- (2026-09-16) A phone shows five nibs, laid out in the same five columns as the crayons and at the same size as them, so the two rows line up under the picture instead of drifting. A wide screen keeps its column of six at the larger size.

- (2026-09-16) The nib row on a phone was left-aligned because an old rule from the earlier paint-box layout was still in the stylesheet and won over the new one. The dead rules are gone and the row centres itself on the picture's width, whether or not it happens to line up with the colors above.

- (2026-09-16) The name page puts air between its letters (letter spacing of about a tenth of the size, counted in the width that decides the size) and thins the outline slightly. Sandbox fonts set the glyphs apart, but the phone's does not, so without the spacing the outlines of neighbouring letters crossed each other on Mikey's screen and not on mine.

- (2026-09-16) The doubled name was two text elements, the fill and the outline, laid out separately: they matched in the sandbox and came out at different widths on Mikey's phone, so the outline drifted across the letters. The name is laid out once now and the outline is a `<use>` copy of that very element, which cannot measure differently. Lesson: never draw the same text twice and expect the two to line up.

- (2026-09-16) The name is now a single outline element drawn last: no white fill underneath, no copy of any kind. Two text elements could drift on a device whose font measures differently from the sandbox's, and a `<use>` copy did not settle it either, so the safest thing is to draw the letters once. Their insides take whatever color the background is given, which is fine and lets a child color inside them.

- (2026-09-16) The login screen carries a build stamp under Contact us, written by build.sh at build time. Three passes were spent on a name-drawing fault that the sandbox never reproduced; a stamp settles in one glance whether the page on a device is the one that was just published.

- (2026-09-16) The name page is back to the drawing Mikey approved: one text element, white letters with their outline painted behind them, no spacing of our own, drawn under the ink rather than over it. Everything tried in between — a second outline element, a `<use>` copy, letter spacing, outline only — made it worse on his devices and looked identical on mine. The build stamp moved to the very foot of the page, under the line about the device.

- (2026-09-16) The coloring page is sized by the height that is really there (`dvh`), so a phone held upright shows the picture, the crayons and the nibs without scrolling. Turned on its side, where there is width to spare and no height, the nibs stand at one side of the picture and the crayons at the other in four columns, and the picture takes nearly the whole height. Those rules sit after the wide-screen ones, because a phone on its side is also a wide screen and would otherwise take the laptop layout.

- (2026-09-16) The coloring page is sized to the window rather than to the reading column: it may use the full width, drops the page's zoom and bottom padding, and the picture takes the height that is left once the title, the bar and the three rows of crayons are accounted for. Measured on a phone upright and sideways, a tablet both ways and a laptop: nothing runs off the bottom at any of them, and the picture is half as large again on a laptop as it was. The title is one line whatever the width, and when a phone is sideways the title bar runs the full width so the restart and close icons sit at the outer edges.

- (2026-09-16) The build stamp reads in Central time, since that is where it is read.
- (2026-09-16) A phone on its side splits down the middle: the title, the bar and the picture take the left, the nibs and the crayons the right. On a laptop or tablet the nibs sit level with the middle of the picture rather than its foot.

- (2026-09-16) The countdown is one shape everywhere: a bar across the top. Standing it upright beside the picture on a sideways phone made it too thin to read, and the title ended up beside the picture rather than above it. Sideways now reads the same way as everywhere else: title across the top with its two icons at the edges, the bar beneath it, then the picture, the nibs and the crayons in one row.
- (2026-09-16) A picture that is filled in rather than drawn on has no nibs, so it shows all twenty crayons in four rows and the paper runs a little below the drawing, which puts the zoom buttons under the picture instead of over it. A drawing picture keeps its square: the hand needs the room.

- (2026-09-16) The coloring page stopped using `dvh` units and `:has()`; a browser a version or two behind ignores both, and when the height rule was ignored the picture fell back to the laptop size and ran off a phone held sideways. Plain `vh` and a class on the wrapper do the same job everywhere. Sideways, the crayons, nibs and zoom buttons are smaller again, and a filled picture (taller than it is wide) takes less width so its height still fits.
- (2026-09-16) The picture's own edge is clipped round, so a filled background no longer paints over the card's border and corners.

- (2026-09-16) A phone held sideways is asked to turn upright rather than shown a squeezed layout, and every sideways rule is gone. The ask only appears on a screen that is short, wide and touched, so tablets and laptops never see it. This is Mikey's call: the app is designed upright, and chasing a sideways phone was costing more than it was worth.
- (2026-09-16) The twenty-first crayon is back, so a wide screen shows three full rows of seven; a phone still shows fifteen, or twenty on a picture that is filled in. The close and start-over icons sit slightly outside the picture's width. On the name page the letters' edge is copied over the ink with `<use>`, so coloring no longer buries it, exactly as it already worked on the drawn pictures.

- (2026-09-16) The name's border stays prominent without a second copy and without fading the crayon: the ink is drawn through a mask that marks the letters' line as out of bounds, so color fills the letters and the paper around them at full strength and stops at the edge instead of running over it. The mask holds a copy of the letters, but it is never drawn, so nothing can read as doubled. The mask protects only the half of the line that is actually visible — the letters are filled inside it, as they are on the page — so the color runs right up to the black edge instead of leaving a white gap beside it.
- (2026-09-16) The doubled letters were never two copies. A line drawn on a letter's outline has two sides; the white fill hides the inner one, so each letter shows a single edge. Take the fill away, or lay a second unfilled copy on top, and both sides show at once, which reads as every letter drawn twice. That explains each attempt that failed, on Mikey's screen and on any screen large enough to see it. The name is white letters with their outline painted behind them, under the ink, and that is where it stays: the edge cannot sit over the ink without losing the fill that makes the letters read properly.
- (2026-09-16) The name is one text element and nothing else: white letters with their outline behind them, drawn under the ink. Three different ways of laying a second copy of the edge over the ink — a second text, a `<use>` of that text, a `<use>` of a plain definition — all rendered cleanly here and came out misaligned on Mikey's device. A crayon crossing a letter therefore covers the line at that point, exactly as it would on paper. If the edge must always show, the alternative is letters with no white fill at all, which colors their insides with whatever the background is given.
- (2026-09-20) A lesson picture follows the logic of the sentence over it. "Red and blue are colors" shows red beside blue, "They are the same" shows two red circles side by side, "Three dots is more than one" shows three beside one. The pair picture (`kind: 'pair'`, halves of any kind) is how a line about two things is drawn, and the lesson-picture test checks each half against the words.
- (2026-09-20) Pre-K is finished before the screen changes shape. The skill folds stay, and a kindergarten course that has been switched on waits out of sight, until every pre-K module is mastered. Before this, finishing pre-K 4 math switched on kindergarten math and the pre-K screen fell back to subject folds. Each skill fold carries a small drawn picture, so a pre-reader can tell Colors from Counting without the word.
- (2026-09-20) A young learner whose screen has emptied for the day sees "Well done! Come back tomorrow." with a speaker, instead of a blank page. A student who came up through the early years never meets a placement check: when the last early module is mastered, any subject that would ask starts at the beginning by itself.
- (2026-09-20) Backups: a file goes to downloads when a student taps Exit (only if something changed), on the first sign-in of each day, and when a course is finished. A browser cannot save a file as it closes, so Exit is the last chance, and nothing is lost either way because every step is already in the browser's own storage. Chrome and Edge on a computer can keep one chosen file rewritten after every practice round for the rest of the visit (`keepOneFile`); the handle is held in memory, never stored, so no new storage call was added. Automatic backups now set the last-backup date.
- (2026-09-20) Student PIN, optional, four digits, chosen by the educator in the add-student popup or from the student's card, asked for on the sign-in screen when that name is tapped. It is a mix-up guard on a shared screen, not a lock on the device, and it is stored on the roster as typed by the educator. Wonder questions can be switched off per student (default on) in the same two places; approval still gates every question, so the switch can never show anything unapproved.
- (2026-09-20) The phone's back button moves up one level inside the app (lesson or picture to courses, courses to sign-in, an educator page to the educator's list) using one guard entry on the browser history; from the sign-in screen, back leaves the app. Speech is scheduled with a ticket: cancel, then speak a beat later, retry once if nothing started; a newer request cancels an older one, which is what stopped a dropped or late line when moving quickly.
- (2026-09-20) Coloring: the line thickness starts in the middle (2.2); a wide screen has four erasers in a column to the right of the picture (paper-colored strokes, so the outline drawn on top is never lost); the zoom-out button pulses while zoomed in; fill pictures on a phone take 50vh with four tighter rows of crayons so nothing scrolls off; the dot-and-line page background shows from 700px, so tablets have it and phones do not. The Let's Color fold has a plain arrow on the right like every other fold, and an educator's walk-through sees every picture unlocked.
- (2026-09-20) My Classroom lists the youngest band first, then by name, and an educator can press and hold a card to drag it into their own order (kept as `order` on the roster). The standards map folds each grade (or subject) behind one counted row. The module story calls a pass a pass and states mastery once, with the date it was earned.
- (2026-09-20) Art electives (grade 3 §117.111, grade 4 §117.114): the strand numbers and anchor codes match the published lists, but each grade maps three of the four TEKS strands (grade 3 lacks 3.3 historical and cultural relevance; grade 4 lacks 4.4 critical evaluation). Left as is pending a fourth module per grade; not a wrong mapping, an incomplete one.
- (2026-09-20) A question whose words repeat the one before it is still spoken: the speech effect keys on the question's position as well as its text. This was the "audio sometimes does not start" report: two questions in a row can draw the same picture and the same sentence, and the old effect saw nothing new to say.
- (2026-09-20) The backup page counts automatic backups: "Last backup today" rather than "Last manual backup", because sign-in and sign-out backups now set the same date.
- (2026-09-20) Pre-K 4 reading gained More big letters (order 5, tracing, stylus): the standing early-years module for this pass.

The popup's state is now `wizardPin` and `wizardWonder`; the browser test now saves a PIN and flips Wonder Questions from the card.

One green Manual backup button replaces Download and Share: it opens the device's share sheet where files can be shared, otherwise it downloads.

Wording on the backup page, the classroom backup tip and the Wonder review page rewritten to Mikey's text of 2026-09-20.

Last backup line in bold; classroom backup tip centered; the Wonder review note about faith sits well below the list.

On an iPad: Share, Add to Home Screen; on Chrome or Edge: the install button in the address bar.
- (2026-09-20, night) The loading screen is the logo drawing itself forward and back (the same `edu-draw` strokes, looped and alternating), both before React mounts (a boot copy of docs/logo.svg in the page shell that shows only while #root is empty) and on the app's own loading screen.
- (2026-09-20, night) Drawing makes a paper sound: noise shaped by a band-pass filter, synthesized on the device by Web Audio, louder as the hand moves faster, started by the touch itself and silent when it stops. No sound file, nothing downloaded. A mouse becomes a paintbrush cursor over a drawing picture only. Pre-K 4 reading gained Trace L, T and F (order 6, tracing): the standing early-years module for this pass.
- (2026-09-21) Let's Play: a second fold in Let's Color's clothes, for games that need no words. One game to start and one more for every course mastered (`gamesUnlocked`), in a fixed order (`GAMES`); a game keeps the coloring clock (five minutes, fifteen resting) by sharing the coloring state under `play:<id>`, and a game writes no event. Kinds: join the dots (star, house, boat, kite, and the student's own name from TRACE_LETTERS), pairs, sorting into two boxes by size or color (drag), a maze carved by a seeded random walk (drag the dot), a jigsaw of one scene (tap two pieces to swap), and Pong. Harder ones wait for a student whose courses reach K, 1 or 2 (`minGrade`). The walk-through sees them all. Rounds are seeded so "play again" is a new shuffle everywhere.

- (2026-09-21, later) Themed matching (pass D, first three): Match the animals (pre-K 3, the sign-in animals), Match the vehicles and Match the things (pre-K 4, coloring outlines). One pair of generators serves every theme (`themedSame`, `themedDifferent`, given a theme from `MATCH_THEMES`); the explanation picture is the pair the words describe, so "Both are a fox" shows two foxes and "The owl is different from the fox" shows the owl beside the fox. Choices `pic:<animal>` and `art:<picture>` draw what the app already draws. More themes need more drawings; those come through the art ledger, not by hand.

No server verifies anything; the device's own yes is the key, the PIN stays as the other one, and it only appears on a secure page (the site or the installed app).

Let's Play sits beneath Let's Color. A student keeps every picture and game they unlocked in pre-K on the kindergarten screen: both counts come from the event log alone, and both folds stay through grade 2.

The method is the Miniature Gladwell arc in `.claude/skills/story-based-learning/SKILL.md`; the cast, the clock (a band is a chapter, characters age faster than the learner), and the rules (wholesome-only for Savanah, Jaxon and Harlow; Georgette is the godmother of Mike's uncle; at most one new core character per story; most stories belong to someone else or no one) are in `docs/CHARACTERS.md`; every picture has an S or C serial and a Leonardo prompt in `docs/ART-REQUESTS.md`. `tests/stories.test.mjs` (check step 5) holds the word limits (200 early years, 350 older), the serials and the cast names.

The offer appears on the student's card in My Classroom ("Finished Kindergarten! Make a certificate / Not now") until it is made or skipped, remembered on the roster (`certificates`), and the report keeps a Certificates card for any finished grade forever, so nothing is a one-time chance. The screen offers four templates drawn in code (Rosette, Stars with one photo, Balloons with two, Gallery with three), a name box, and photo slots; Print prints only the sheet, sideways; Save picture draws the sheet onto a canvas and downloads a PNG (Share stays off, as on the backup page). ## 2026-09-24 (pass CI): the printed book with paintings, grade 3 batch 1, pictures checked against their words

- Mikey's two notes: the Let's Read card on the Story Log is deep green with a white title, so it stands apart from the student cards, and it never prints with the log. They stay at or under 260 words so two still share a printed page before any painting arrives. Choices: the main-idea boy is Mateo with his abuela; the tadpoles grow back legs at about six weeks, not two; the clay garden is fixed with compost, not sand, which can make clay worse; the prefix story uses a word creature in costumes so no picture needs legible writing; closing pictures show the idea of the last paragraph rather than the cast smiling.
- The printed book with paintings (Mikey: no huge gaps, and a picture never on a different page from the words it shows; the design is my call). A painted story may now take more than one sheet. printParts in logic.mjs splits it between paragraphs, never between a paragraph and its picture, using sizes measured from a real print (PRINT_PAGE). On paper a painted picture floats beside its own paragraph at two fifths of the width, sides alternating, the main painting prints about half wide, and every painting prints four by three whatever shape it arrives in, so the estimate holds. On the grade 3 fractions book with all thirty pictures pretend-painted, three of five stories fill two sheets at 86 to 87 percent and two need a short third sheet; nothing is cut off. The sizes get re-measured when real paintings arrive. Print or Save now loads every painting before printing, since pictures load lazily on screen, and one story printed from its own page or the Story Log prints in place instead of being lifted out, so a two-sheet story keeps its second sheet.
- Pictures checked against their words (Mikey): tools/story-art-audit.mjs lists every picture whose description shares no meaningful word with the paragraph it follows (for the main picture, the title and first two paragraphs), in docs/STORY-ART-AUDIT.md. A one-time pass moved 151 pictures that sat one paragraph away from the words they show, keeping the picture rules. 132 of 1,706 stay flagged, mostly in stories not yet doubled (their pictures are rewritten when the doubling reaches them) and closing pictures after an echo paragraph. A synonym can fool the check, so a person reads each row; it is a reading aid, not a test yet.
- Long stories join the doubling queue (Mikey): 400 words and six pictures each, done after each grade's short stories. STORY-LENGTH.md lists all 62. The stories test still caps them at 350 words and three paragraphs; that loosens with the first doubled long story.
- Planned, not built: a game attached to every course with many more kinds and the same kind held far apart, and a coloring page placeholder with a Leonardo prompt for every early-years module. Both are next passes, one piece per pass beside each story batch.

- Story pictures on screen (Mikey: pictures and their words side by side, alternating sides, sliding in as you scroll). From 720px wide, each picture sits beside its paragraph at two fifths of the width, sides alternating, and paragraphs without a picture run the full width. Phones keep the picture under its paragraph: beside a picture, a phone's column of words runs about twenty characters a line, too short for a young reader to track. Each picture eases into place once as it scrolls into view (IntersectionObserver adds edu-in; a picture beside words comes in from its own side, a stacked one rises), never loops, and is simply in place under reduced motion and in print. Native lazy loading already downloads a picture only as it nears the screen. The class is edu-par-pic, because edu-side was already the fixed side art and its display none hid every picture on the first try.
- Audit after the batch fixes: 153 pictures moved one paragraph to the paragraph they show, and 127 of 1,706 are still flagged. About 50 of those are early-years stories, many of them letter and number pictures whose descriptions spell the letters, which the word check cannot match; they are reviewed next pass.
- Standing direction (Mikey): he says continue and the next pass starts from the checkpoint and docs/HANDOFF.md. Claude also works as the education expert (research-backed technique, his fun, concise, clever tone with sentence lengths varied on purpose) and as the motion designer (Leonardo.ai-like movement, judged against distraction for young learners). New code gets plain-language comments. A security pass comes at the end, before the paintings.

Fixed while doubling: the paper boat now gives all seven real folds (the old four steps only made a hat); the rule-or-law story no longer says a walk signal law comes from Congress (it is a Texas law, made by the legislature in Austin); the puppy story no longer states how much water a puppy needs (the girl measures what hers drank); pictures no longer ask Leonardo to draw numbers or signs; repeated names across grade 3 were changed so each story has its own kid.
- Early-years pictures: 49 pictures in pre-K to grade 2 were rewritten to show the paragraph they sit beside, as scenes rather than letterforms wherever the old description asked Leonardo to draw letters. The audit now flags 77 of 1,760, none in pre-K through grade 3.
- License (Mikey did not object to the proposed wording): LICENSE.md now forbids using any part to train, fine-tune, test, evaluate or prompt an AI system or to build a dataset for one, and forbids scraping, crawling, mirroring or bulk downloading; NOTICE.md says the same in one line. A robots.txt waits for a custom domain, because crawlers only read it at a domain's root.

## 2026-09-24 (pass CO): a game for every course

- Mikey asked for a game attached to every course, the same kind held far apart as courses finish, and many more kinds. COURSE_GAMES in logic.mjs now gives each of the 81 courses one or two games, chosen by subject and grade; finishing the course unlocks them (unlockedGameIds: the starter, the first game on a student's list, and the games of finished courses). Let's Play lists the games of a student's own courses, and the educator walk-through opens them all. Star is the starter game, open from the first day.

Advice given: new endings load in every browser and take HTTPS like any other, but some school web filters distrust new endings, and the name EduSphere is already used by several education products, including an adaptive learning platform, so a new name with its own .com is worth choosing before launch. Mikey decides; the name is not changed in the app.
