A round is never shortened, so each was grown to at least its round length plus two, every new answer taught in its lesson (tools/untaught.mjs and the rules test agree): grade 9 theme development, grade 10 paraphrasing and symbols, grade 11 satire and enough evidence, grade 12 assumptions, college correlation and causation and numbers in prose (two new cases each, every case asking two questions), grade 9 homeostasis and grade 12 human impact (one bank each now asks several questions from the same lesson: the thermostat, homeostasis, diabetes; carbon dioxide, ultraviolet light, the ozone hole slowly closing, algae, eight billion people, why cleared forests leave more carbon dioxide), the three grade 10 history modules (Augustus, Julius Caesar, Athens and Rome by date, the church over all, perhaps a third of Europe, fewer workers, Gutenberg, Greek and Roman learning, Italy, Napoleon, three thousand miles, the 1600s and 1700s, 1850, power from the governed), and kindergarten what plants need (a rock, a magnet and a nail it does not need, beside the water and sun it does). docs/QUESTION-POOLS.md: 0 of 603 modules short.
- Two small fixes the checks found on the way: the paraphrase explanation lowercased the whole lost fact, which would have printed december; it now lowers only the first letter. An older generator test kept its own list of known icons; it now includes the eight drawn since.
- Closing a game came back to the top of the page (Mikey). The overview already saved the spot, but restored it once, before the page had finished growing back, so the browser clamped it short. The scroll is now retried for up to a second until it holds.
- Games that move on their own (the space shooter, Catch and Pong) open behind a Begin cover on the board's own paper, so nothing starts before a child is ready and How to play can be read first; Play again starts at once. Frog Jumps and Buckets were checked too: nothing in them moves until a child drags, so they open as before. The layout audit (tests/e2e/game-layout.mjs, run on request) sees the cover first on those three.
- Not done yet: Grow the Plant's rounds, so each plant shows one problem a child can see (droopy and dry, pale and leaning toward a window, roots in an empty pot) and the child reads the plant to choose its need. That is the next pass.

## 2026-10-03 (pass IV): Grow the Plant asks a child to read the plant

- Mikey has not committed IS, IT or IU, so the commit message covers IS to IV.
- Mikey asked whether Grow the Plant was too easy with water, soil and sun right every round. It was: after the first plant, a child tapped the same three pictures from memory. Now each of four plants shows one problem, said aloud and drawn: droopy with dry, pale soil needs water; pale and leaning toward a window needs sunshine; roots with no soil around them need soil. Only the need that fixes the problem grows the plant. A toy wobbles (A toy cannot help a plant grow), and a wrong need sends the child back to the evidence (Look again: the plant is droopy, and the soil is dry) without giving the answer away. Every round shows all three problems at least once, so the child has to read each plant.
- The science, kept to what holds: a plant short of water wilts and its soil dries out; a plant short of light grows pale and stretches toward the light; roots need soil around them. The game still uses the three needs a child can give with a tap; the lesson's other two, air and room, are already there.
- Tests: a rules test holds every round to all three problems, and the browser walk plays the game the new way (a toy and a wrong need change nothing; the right need grows each of the four plants).

## 2026-10-03 (pass IW): Wonder questions for teens and adults, failure and hard feelings first

- Mikey has not committed IS to IV, so the commit message covers IS to IW. His phone review list finished with IV, so the Wonder pool program resumes, with the older stages this time and his authors.
- Twenty new questions, listed here for approval; none reaches a student until an educator approves it in the app.
- Teen, failure: You got a test back covered in red marks. Did you read the mistakes, or shove it in your bag? Why? / You froze when it was your turn to speak, and now the next time scares you even more. How does anyone get braver? / Before a big project, imagine it is already finished and it failed. What went wrong? / You got a bad grade and hid it from your family. Now it feels worse than the grade did. What now?
- Teen, feelings: The writer Michael Singer says you are not the voice in your head, but the one who hears it. What would change if that were true? / The psychologist Rick Hanson says the brain is like Velcro for bad experiences and Teflon for good ones. Is that true of yours? / Something embarrassing happened weeks ago, and your mind keeps replaying it. Why does it do that, and how do you make it stop? / Your best friend is moving far away. How do you say goodbye, and how do you stay friends?
- Teen, hard days: The Roman emperor Marcus Aurelius wrote in his private notebook that what stands in the way can become the way. Is something in your way right now? / You woke up in a bad mood for no clear reason. Do you have to stay in it all day?
- Grown, failure: A Princeton professor named Johannes Haushofer published a CV of his failures: the programs, jobs and grants he did not get. What would yours list, and why might sharing it help someone? / Years from now, which will you regret more: a try that failed, or a try you never made? / You have put two years into something that is not working. Is the time you already spent a reason to keep going? / One study found people learned as much from other people's failures as from their successes, but less from their own. Whose failures have taught you the most?
- Grown, feelings: Michael Singer asks readers to imagine the voice in their head as a roommate who talks all day long. Would you keep a roommate like that? / Rick Hanson argues that lingering on good experiences for a few extra seconds helps them stick. Is noticing the good a real skill, or a way of fooling yourself? / When did you last feel awe, and what did it do to the worries you brought with you? / You sent a message and heard nothing back for a day. What story did you tell yourself about why?
- Grown, hard days: Viktor Frankl, a psychiatrist who survived the Nazi camps, wrote that one freedom can never be taken away: the freedom to choose your attitude. Do you believe that? / On a heavy day, does moving your body change your mind?
- The research behind the claims, read before use: learning less from one's own failure (Eskreis-Winkler and Fishbach, Psychological Science 2019; a 2025 commentary argues the task confounds consistency with learning, so both questions say to hold it loosely); regret (Gilovich and Medvec 1994 and 1995, with the 1998 resolution with Kahneman that many long-term regrets are wistful); exercise and depression (Noetel and others, BMJ 2024, 218 trials, with the authors' own caution about trial quality); awe and the small self (Piff and others, Journal of Personality and Social Psychology 2015: the eucalyptus grove and the drops of colored water in milk); Johannes Haushofer's CV of failures (2016, crediting Melanie Stefan's 2010 Nature article); the sunk cost effect (Arkes and Blumer 1985); the liking gap (Boothby and others, Psychological Science 2018); rumination (Nolen-Hoeksema); secrets weighing through mind-wandering (Slepian and others 2017); self-distancing and self-talk by name (Kross and others); inner speech varying between people (Nedergaard and Lupyan 2024); exposure in small steps; desirable difficulties.
- Mikey's authors, attributed and exact: Viktor Frankl (the last of the human freedoms, to choose one's attitude, from Man's Search for Meaning, quoted only in those six words and read against the book's passage); Marcus Aurelius (what stands in the way can become the way, a paraphrase of Meditations 5.20, his private notebook); Aristotle (we become brave by doing brave acts, Nicomachean Ethics II); Michael Singer (you are not the voice but the one who hears it; the inner roommate; letting the noise pass, from The Untethered Soul); Rick Hanson (Velcro and Teflon; a few extra seconds with the good, from Hardwiring Happiness). The often quoted Frankl line about a space between stimulus and response is not used: it is not in his books.
- Honest limits, said in the questions themselves: the failure study is a lead, not a law; bold claims about rewiring the brain run ahead of the savoring evidence; Frankl's freedom is not a test that struggling people have failed; exercise sits alongside therapy and medication, not instead of them; a bad mood that keeps returning for weeks is worth telling a trusted adult, and a heavy season deserves a doctor or counselor.
- The pool: 319 questions (early 83, growing 76, teen 72, grown 88). One per module would take 284 more (early 84, growing 59, teen 56, grown 85).

## 2026-10-03 (pass IX): Wonder questions for younger learners, failure and hard feelings first, and no more venting advice

- Mikey committed through IW, so the commit message starts again at IX. This chat (The Wise Human 3.0) was restarted from its first message, which still carried the IR zip; Mikey then attached the IW zip, and IX is built on it.
- Twenty-four new questions, listed here for Mikey's approval; like every Wonder question, none reaches a student until an educator approves it in the app. Early questions are shared from pre-K 3 to grade 2, so each had to make sense to a three-year-old and an eight-year-old, with choices short enough to hold in mind when heard.
- Early, failure: Your drawing came out different from the picture in your head. Is it a bad drawing? / You said a word wrong, and someone giggled. What do you do? / Your paper airplane crashed right away. What do you do next? / You broke your friend's toy by accident. What do you do? / You tried to help wash the dishes, and water went everywhere. Was helping a mistake?
- Early, feelings: You woke up from a scary dream. What helps you feel safe again? / A thunderstorm is booming outside. What helps when you feel scared? / You need a shot at the doctor. What helps you feel brave? / Someone grabbed your toy without asking. What do you do? / A fun trip got canceled because of the rain. What helps the sad feeling?
- Early, hard days: Your morning went wrong: spilled cereal and a lost shoe. Will the whole day be bad? / Waiting for something you really want feels so long. What helps you wait?
- Growing, failure: You finished your homework and then left it at home. What would help next time? / You blamed a friend for something, and then found out they did not do it. What now? / You told a small lie to stay out of trouble, and now you have to keep remembering it. Was it worth it? / You want to quit your team or your lessons in the middle of the season. Is quitting the same as failing? / You were sure your project would take one evening, and it took three. Why does everything take longer than we think?
- Growing, feelings: Some people say hitting a pillow gets your anger out. Does it? / After you do something wrong, do you think "I did a bad thing" or "I am a bad kid"? Is there a difference? / Your friend won the prize you wanted. Can you feel happy for them and sad for you at the same time? / Some people say crying is for babies. Is it? / Is waiting for something scary, like the dentist or a big test, sometimes worse than the thing itself?
- Growing, hard days: A test, a fight with a friend and a sick pet, all in one week. What do you do when hard things pile up? / At the end of a hard day, can you still find three good things?
- Owned and fixed, three answers that recommended venting a feeling. My own artist line in pass IS (w-early-grown-up-said-no) said feelings need somewhere to go and suggested stomping out a drum beat or squeezing a pillow, and two older lines framed drawing as getting a feeling out (w-early-feeling-sad: the feeling has somewhere to go; w-angry-feeling: getting it out of your body). The evidence points the other way: in Bushman's 2002 study, angered people who hit a punching bag while thinking about whoever angered them ended up angrier and more aggressive than people who did nothing, and a 2024 review of 154 studies (Kjaervik and Bushman, Clinical Psychology Review) found that calming activities reduce anger while arousing ones mostly do not, jogging least of all. With children aged 6 to 12, drawing something new lifted a sad mood more than drawing what had upset them (Drake and Winner, Cognition and Emotion 2013). New text, for approval: w-early-grown-up-said-no: "When I feel cross, I draw something I love, like a garden or my dog, and my busy hands help my mind settle. In one study, children who drew something new felt better than children who drew what had upset them." / w-early-feeling-sad: "Some people draw something they love when they feel sad, like a pet or a favorite place. In one study, that helped children feel better more than drawing the sad thing did." / w-angry-feeling: "Putting a feeling on paper turns it into something you can look at and name, instead of something that pushes you around.". A rules test now reads every Wonder voice for venting advice, and CLAUDE.md carries the rule.
- The research behind the claims, read before use: needles (Birnie and others, Cochrane 2018: 59 trials, 5,550 children; distraction, breathing, hypnosis and combined CBT help, with low-quality evidence, which the question says); helping (Warneken and Tomasello, Science 2006: 18-month-olds help a stranger without being asked or rewarded); intent and accidents (Cushman and others, Cognition 2013: intent-based judgment grows between 4 and 8, accidents judged leniently by about 8); speech sounds (Crowe and McLeod 2020: most English consonants by 5, the th in thumb at about 6); bad dreams (the AAP and AASM: most common from about 3 to 6, up to half of young children wake a parent); thunder (National Weather Service: five seconds per mile, when thunder roars, go indoors); waiting (Mischel and Ebbesen 1970 and Mischel, Ebbesen and Zeiss 1972; Kidd, Palmeri and Aslin 2013: about 12 minutes against 3 with a reliable grown-up; Watts, Duncan and Quan 2018: the link to later life about half the original and two thirds smaller after controls); the checklist (Haynes and others, NEJM 2009: eight hospitals, complications 11 to 7 percent, deaths 1.5 to 0.8 percent, a before and after study); the planning fallacy (Buehler, Griffin and Ross 1994: 33.9 days guessed, 55.5 taken, fewer than half done by the worst-case guess; unpacking a task into steps helps, Kruger and Evans 2004); lying (Talwar and Lee: most children peek and many deny it, and younger ones blurt out the toy's name); quitting (Angela Duckworth's Hard Thing Rule, Grit, pages 241 and 242); guilt and shame (Stuewig, Tangney and others 2015: 380 fifth graders followed into young adulthood); mixed feelings (Larsen, To and Fireman, Psychological Science 2007, ages 5 to 12); crying (Bylsma, Vingerhoets and Rottenberg 2008: 5,096 students in 35 countries; comfort helps a cry help, shame and suppression make it less likely; about a third report no change); dread (Berns and others, Science 2006: most chose a shock sooner, and 9 of 32 took a stronger one sooner rather than wait); a bedtime to-do list (Scullin and others 2018: 57 young adults fell asleep faster); three good things (Seligman and others 2005, with White and others, PLOS ONE 2019: effects smaller than first reported); kintsugi; the Wright brothers' 1901 wind tunnel (about two hundred wing shapes) and Wilbur's 1908 admission to the Aero-Club de France that in 1901 he had told Orville people would not fly for fifty years; Judith Viorst's Alexander and the Terrible, Horrible, No Good, Very Bad Day (1972), paraphrased.
- One of Mikey's thinkers, exact: Socrates, paraphrased from Plato's Apology 21d (wiser in one small way: he did not think he knew what he did not know). The popular line "I know that I know nothing" is not in Plato and is not used.
- Said honestly in the questions themselves: the needle studies were not perfect; waiting well at four does not decide a life; the three good things effect is smaller than first reported, worth a try and not magic; crying does not help about a third of the time; the to-do list finding "seems to" work. For Mikey, not in the text: the checklist study compared before and after rather than randomizing, and the guilt and shame study shows a link, not proof of cause.
- The pool: 343 questions (early 95, growing 88, teen 72, grown 88); failure and feelings now early 55 and growing 49. One per module would take 260 more (early 72, growing 47, teen 56, grown 85).
- Checks: rules 1,897 (one new test: no Wonder voice recommends venting), coverage 366, spelling 77, stories 6,249; check.sh ALL CHECKS PASSED in one run (browser click-through 301, license 7, drag 7, educator 12, back 21, dark 55, standalone page 8). Every new text was also read by a script for sentences over 32 words, voice sentences under five words, spoken lines over 20 words, dashes and duplicate prompts: none.
- Next: the game cards read-through (Myth or Fact, Valid or Not, Reason or Not, Green or Red Flag, the decks), then the next Wonder batch, then PSYC 2301.

## 2026-10-03 (pass IY): the game cards read by hand

- Mikey: when he says only "continue", the pass is uncommitted; he will say when he commits. The commit message covers IX and IY.
- Every card with words was read by hand: Myth or Fact (10 cards), Green Flag or Red Flag (12), Valid or Not (8 argument pairs in 4 forms), Reason or Not (8 claims, 16 reasons), Sort the Because (6 claims, 12 reasons, 8 fakes), and the decks: 23 pairs decks, 25 rule decks, 9 order decks, 4 find-the-evidence decks, 3 fix-it decks, 3 build-a-sentence decks, the color mixing decks, Which Sense, Fit the Room, Ask the Right Question, Who Gains, Find the Tool and Sort the Ledger. Dates, symbols, formulas, products, roots and capitals all checked out. What changed:
- Sort the Because gave its answers away: every fake began with Because and no real reason did, so a child could tap the two without it and never read the claim. Every choice now starts with Because, as the lesson frames a reason (a reason is what comes after because); the buttons read the text as it is, and a rules test holds every choice to the form. New rule in CLAUDE.md: in any card game, right and wrong choices share one form.
- Valid or Not read stiffly ("It is not the case that the street is wet") and made a double negative from the dead battery ("It is not the case that the car will not start"). Each argument pair now carries its own plain negations ("The street is not wet. So it is not raining."); a rules test keeps every line to one negative.
- The card text changes, old then new, for approval:
  - Myth or Fact: other things lower STI risk too (abstinence, vaccines); among birth control methods, condoms are the one that does: 'Only condoms lower STI risk.' becomes 'Pills help prevent pregnancy, not STIs; condoms lower STI risk.'
  - Myth or Fact: "most" overstated what the data show; "many" matches its own fact card ("often someone the victim knows"): 'Most victims are recruited by someone they trust.' becomes 'Many victims are recruited by someone they know or trust.'
  - Reason or Not: "can" read as a verb out of context: 'The nearest can is a five-minute walk from the swings.' becomes 'The nearest trash can is a five-minute walk from the swings.'
  - Reason or Not: "that day" pointed at its own claim: 'Most players have no other clubs that day.' becomes 'Most players have no other clubs on Tuesdays.'
  - Reason or Not: "it" pointed at its own claim: 'Students line up outside it after school.' becomes 'Students line up outside the library after school.'
  - Reason or Not: "one" pointed at its own claim: 'The drama club has copies of one already.' becomes 'The drama club already has copies of a play.'
  - Reason or Not: "early" was vague, and the reason now names why (fiber): 'Students who eat fruit early stay full longer.' becomes 'Fruit has fiber, which helps students feel full longer.'
  - Sort the Because: 'Kids eat more when they have already run around.', 'The playground is empty before lunch.' becomes 'Because kids eat more when they have already run around.', 'Because the playground is empty before lunch.'
  - Sort the Because: a pet fish does not set off food allergies; what matters is that it has no fur: 'Nobody in the class is allergic to fish.', 'A fish can stay at school over the weekend.' becomes 'Because a fish has no fur to set off allergies.', 'Because a fish can stay at school over the weekend.'
  - Sort the Because: 'That corner gets sun all day.', 'The cafeteria could use the vegetables.' becomes 'Because that corner gets sun all day.', 'Because the cafeteria could use the vegetables.'
  - Sort the Because: 'Students sit on the floor at lunch reading.', 'There are six chairs for twenty readers.' becomes 'Because students sit on the floor at lunch reading.', 'Because there are six chairs for twenty readers.'
  - Sort the Because: 'The science unit this month is about water.', 'The river is a ten-minute bus ride.' becomes 'Because the science unit this month is about water.', 'Because the river is a ten-minute bus ride.'
  - Sort the Because: 'Reading aloud helps with new words.', 'Friday afternoons have no other lessons.' becomes 'Because reading aloud helps with new words.', 'Because Friday afternoons have no other lessons.'
  - vocabulary: an adjective matched to an adjective: ['abundant', 'plenty'] becomes ['abundant', 'plentiful']
  - health: MyPlate makes half the plate fruits and vegetables, as the health quick check already says: ['vegetables', 'half the plate'] becomes ['fruits and vegetables', 'half the plate']
  - health: the lesson's own words (its light tells the brain it is daytime) instead of an untaught picture: ['screen light', 'a small sun'] becomes ['screen light', 'tells the brain it is day']
  - health: germs, plural (the acid from many bacteria): ['cavity', 'a hole a germ made'] becomes ['cavity', 'a hole made by germs']
  - science 6: two oxygen atoms joined are still an element: ['compound', 'atoms joined'] becomes ['compound', 'different atoms joined']
  - science 11: a change of direction is acceleration too: ['acceleration', 'speed changing'] becomes ['acceleration', 'velocity changing']
  - acid or base: a battery is not an acid (it holds one); grapefruit is: 'lime', 'battery'] becomes 'lime', 'grapefruit']
  - noun or verb: "a cook" and "a laugh" are everyday nouns too; write and carry are plainly verbs: 'cook', 'laugh'] becomes 'write', 'carry']
  - solving 2x + 3 = 11: natural wording: see the plus 3 was done last becomes notice that adding 3 came last
  - evidence, grade 3: workers can lay eggs too when a hive loses its queen: The queen lays all the eggs. becomes The queen lays the eggs.
  - evidence, grade 5: estimates of how long range widely and are not well measured: Plastic bags can take hundreds of years to break down. becomes Plastic bags break down very slowly, over many years.
  - evidence, grade 9: the review found a plateau, not a fade: the benefit fades when students spend more than about two hours a night. becomes the benefit levels off past about two hours a night.
  - Which Sense: a rose can be seen too; every other card names its sense: ['a rose', 'smell'] becomes ['a sweet-smelling rose', 'smell']
  - Who Gains: the bacteria fix nitrogen from the air; they do not make it: roots make nitrogen for the plant and get sugar from it. becomes roots feed the plant nitrogen from the air and get sugar back.
- Open item, found in the read-through and left for its own pass because it touches many lessons: the lessons call ( ) brackets, as British English does; American math, the TEKS (5.4E: parentheses and brackets) and Common Core (5.OA.A.1) call ( ) parentheses and [ ] brackets. The order deck keeps "brackets" until the lessons change with it.

The other steps passed in the full run: license 7, drag 7, educator 12, back 21, dark 55, standalone page 8.

## 2026-10-03 (pass IZ): Wonder questions for teens and adults, from Mikey's authors and newer research

- Mikey said only "continue", so IX, IY and IZ are uncommitted and the commit message covers all three.
- Sixteen new questions, listed here for approval; none reaches a student until an educator approves it in the app. Before writing, every teen and grown question already in the pool was read so none repeats: Deutsch's problems are soluble, Popper's guesses and mistakes, Naval's desire as a contract, Brooks's satisfaction fraction and his two kinds of intelligence, loneliness in a crowded room, and guilt and shame are already there.
- Teen, failure: You reread your notes three times and felt ready, but the test went badly. Why can feeling ready fool you? / You promised to help a friend with something important, and you forgot. What makes an apology actually repair it? / You were building a new habit and missed three days in a row. Is the habit ruined?
- Teen, feelings: Scrolling through other people's best moments, do you ever end up feeling worse about your own life? Why might that happen? / You found out people were talking about you when you were not there. Does that mean they were saying something mean? / A friend you used to be close to has drifted away. Does that mean the friendship failed?
- Teen, hard days: Arthur Brooks, who studies happiness, suggests keeping your phone out of the bedroom and off for the first hour after you wake. What would a week of that do to your mornings? / The Roman emperor Marcus Aurelius wrote a note to himself to begin the morning by expecting ungrateful and difficult people. Is expecting a hard day gloomy, or wise?
- Grown, failure: For fifty years, students learned that animals given shocks they could not escape learned to be helpless. In 2016 the two scientists behind that idea wrote that they had it backward. What changes if they were right? / You made a careful decision, and it turned out badly. Was it a bad decision? / Your first-choice college said no. How much does that decide about your future?
- Grown, feelings: Something hard happened and you have not told anyone. Could writing about it, just for yourself, help? / Is happiness a good thing to aim for, or can chasing it make you less happy? / Arthur Brooks suggests practicing boredom in stretches of fifteen minutes or more, with no phone. Why might anyone want to feel bored on purpose?
- Grown, hard days: Viktor Frankl wrote that when a situation can no longer be changed, the challenge becomes changing ourselves. What would that look like in a situation you cannot change? / Marcus Aurelius wrote in his private notebook that the soul is dyed by its thoughts. What color are your thoughts dyeing yours lately?
- Mikey's thinkers, read before use and paraphrased: Aristotle's three kinds of friendship (Nicomachean Ethics VIII.3); Marcus Aurelius, Meditations 2.1 (begin the morning expecting the busybody, the ungrateful, the arrogant; they act from ignorance of good and evil; we are made for cooperation, like feet and hands) and 5.16 (the soul is dyed by the thoughts), both in George Long's translation; Viktor Frankl, Man's Search for Meaning (when a situation cannot be changed, we are challenged to change ourselves, with his example of an incurable illness; happiness cannot be pursued, it must ensue); Arthur Brooks (boredom and the default mode network; no phone in the bedroom, none in the first hour after waking, practice stretches of fifteen minutes and longer). A misattribution caught and taught: "We are what we repeatedly do" is Will Durant's 1926 summary of Aristotle, not Aristotle's words.
- The research, read before use: retrieval practice (Roediger and Karpicke, Psychological Science 2006: rereading won at five minutes, recall practice won a week later, 61 to 40 percent, and the rereaders predicted the opposite); apologies (Lewicki, Polin and Lount 2016: 755 people; acknowledging responsibility mattered most, an offer of repair second, asking forgiveness least); habits (Lally and others, European Journal of Social Psychology 2010: 96 volunteers, a middle estimate of 66 days, a range of 18 to 254, one missed day did not matter); passive scrolling (Verduyn and others 2015, young adults, through envy) and its limits for teens (Beyens and others, Scientific Reports 2020: after passive use 10 percent felt worse, 46 percent better, 44 percent no different); gossip (Robbins and Karan 2019: 467 people with recorders, about 52 minutes a day, about 15 percent negative, three quarters neutral); learned helplessness at fifty (Maier and Seligman, Psychological Review 2016: passivity is the default, control is what is learned; animal studies, with critics); resulting (Annie Duke, Thinking in Bets 2018, Super Bowl XLIX); college selectivity (Dale and Krueger 2002 and 2014, with their exceptions, and Chetty, Deming and Friedman 2023: Ivy-Plus raised the chance of the top 1 percent by 60 percent, nearly doubled elite graduate school, tripled prestigious firms, with much smaller effects on average earnings); expressive writing (Pennebaker and Beall 1986; Frattaroli 2006, 146 studies, a small effect; distress often rises first); valuing happiness (Mauss and others, Emotion 2011, women only).
- Said honestly in the questions: the habit study only showed one missed day does not matter; the scrolling effect differs from teen to teen; the helplessness work is from animals and still questioned; the writing benefit is small and can hurt before it helps, with a pointer to a trusted person or counselor; the happiness study is one line of research; whether phones starve the default mode network is open.
- The pool: 359 questions (early 95, growing 88, teen 80, grown 96). One per module would take 244 more (early 72, growing 47, teen 48, grown 77).
- Checks: rules 1,899, coverage 366, spelling 77, stories 6,249; check.sh ALL CHECKS PASSED in one run (browser click-through 301, license 7, drag 7, educator 12, back 21, dark 55, standalone page 8). Every new text was also read by a script for sentences over 32 words, voice sentences under five words, dashes and duplicate prompts; two four-word sentences were lengthened.
- Next: General Psychology (PSYC 2301); the parentheses pass and more Wonder batches whenever Mikey wants them.

## 2026-10-03 (pass JA): parentheses, not brackets

- Mikey committed through IZ, so the commit message starts again at JA. He chose this pass before new content: accuracy first.
- The lessons called ( ) brackets, as British English does. American math, the TEKS (5.4E: parentheses and brackets; 5.4F: up to two levels of grouping) and Common Core (5.OA.A.1: parentheses, brackets, or braces) call ( ) parentheses and [ ] brackets. Every lesson, key idea, worked example, quick check, explanation, deck, story and picture caption now says parentheses for ( ); where a factored form was meant, it now says factors or binomials, which is the exact math word.
- Grade 5's order of operations had taught only one level of grouping, though TEKS 5.4F asks for two. It now teaches brackets as the outside pair, worked from the inside out (2 x [10 - (3 + 4)] = 6), and its practice round gains a question of that kind (g5-two-levels), whose wrong choices are the two usual slips. The PEMDAS line had listed brackets and powers while naming PEMDAS; it now spells out Parentheses, Exponents, Multiply and Divide, Add and Subtract, and says exponents arrive in grade 6, as TEKS 5.4F keeps them out of grade 5.
- The lesson and question edits, for approval:
  - order deck: steps: ['brackets', 'exponents', 'multiply and divide', 'add and subtract'] becomes steps: ['parentheses', 'exponents', 'multiply and divide', 'add and subtract']
  - multi-step equations: 'Brackets come first. Before you gather anything, multiply out the bracket: becomes 'Parentheses come first. Before you gather anything, multiply the 3 by each term inside the parentheses:
  - multi-step equations key idea: First, clear any brackets. becomes First, clear any parentheses.
  - factoring: 'Multiplying two brackets gives a trinomial: becomes 'Multiplying two binomials gives a trinomial:

- Four lessons, deeper than the grade 9 course: Methods and measurement (hypothesis, operational definition, descriptive methods, correlation from -1 to +1 and the third variable, experiments with random assignment, control groups and double-blind design, reliability, validity and norms, the 2015 replication project, research ethics and the review board); Where psychology came from (Wundt 1879, Titchener, James 1890, Freud, Gestalt, Watson 1913, Skinner, the cognitive revolution, Maslow and Rogers, Washburn 1894, Calkins 1905, Sumner 1920, the Clarks' doll studies with the caution that later researchers questioned their methods); One behavior, seven lenses (the seven perspectives on one case of test anxiety, and the biopsychosocial model); Brain, body and behavior (neurons, the all-or-none action potential, five neurotransmitters, the nervous system's divisions, the four lobes, the hippocampus, amygdala and cerebellum, and stress hormones). Sixty-two questions, every answer named in its lesson. One question was reworded because the wording check read "Brown v. Board?" as a fragment.
- Facts checked by web this pass: the Open Science Collaboration's 2015 project (100 studies; 97 percent of the originals and 36 percent of the repeats significant; effects about half as large; its leader cautioned that a failed repeat does not prove a finding false); the dates and firsts above; Sumner teaching Kenneth Clark at Howard; the Brown footnote. Ulric Neisser's 1967 book could not be confirmed in time and was left out.
- Game: Cause or Correlation, the fifty-ninth kind. Each card describes a study, and the student taps Experiment (chance chose the groups) or Correlation (things were only measured); a rules test holds that every experiment card names chance and no correlation card does. Its screen is the Myth or Fact screen with its own words, and the click-through plays a full round.
- Stories (S3572 to S3595, CS595 to CS600, ledger rows and prompts written): Clever Hans (Pfungst's 1907 tests, 89 against 6 percent), Mary Whiton Calkins and the degree Harvard refused, a nursing student's test anxiety through seven lenses, and H.M. and the mirror-drawn star. The long story gives Savanah (C8) a sonography student's General Psychology class: a confounded first try, a coin flip for forty classmates, a blind grader, a replication of Roediger and Karpicke's 2006 result, a debriefing, and a racing heart on an echo monitor.
- Wonder: two grown questions, on the replication project and on which lens explains your own behavior. The grown pool is 98, the whole pool 361.

A lesson can now set `prose: true` to keep its paragraphs whole, and rewritten lessons use it. The default is unchanged until Mikey has seen the new style.

Two "show me another way" panels follow the flow (Maya's Saturday, and heaters when demand and supply rise together). The bank grew from 15 to 21 questions in natural sentences, eleven of them with a setup line, including the ones Mikey named: "When the price of something rises, what usually happens to the amount people want to buy?" (it falls), "What do economists call the price where the amount buyers want matches the amount sellers offer?" and the frontier moving outward with a reason. Every short answer is taught (untaught 0 of 615).
- The pin story, retold. It now says who Adam Smith was and what The Wealth of Nations is (his 1776 book asking why some countries grow rich), walks through the steps of pin making, gives Smith's claim about the untrained worker, then the historians' check, then Ricardo, with no colons. It runs 350 words, the most the story rule allows; the pictures and their captions are unchanged and still share words with their paragraphs.

The economics is standard textbook content, and every number in the examples was worked by hand (8 loaves and 3 scarves split evenly, 9 and 3 specialized).

The longest clip is 508 characters and the longest whole story 2,751. Each clip opens with a short direction for its age band (pre-K to grade 2 warm and gentle, drawing out key words; grades 3 to 5 warm and friendly; 6 to 8 warm and natural; 9 up warm and conversational), kept short because ElevenLabs counts tags as characters. Questions and Wonder questions are not played from recordings yet; their direction (warm, pleasant, mildly upbeat) is written down for when they are.
- tools/audio-generate.mjs makes the audio with one command (ELEVENLABS_API_KEY in the environment, --voice and --voice-young). For each missing clip it calls ElevenLabs' Create speech endpoint with model eleven_v4 and the plain words before and after the clip (previous_text and next_text, which ElevenLabs documents for steady delivery across chunks), and saves audio/<key>.mp3. It skips finished clips, so it can stop and start. The endpoint, the model ID eleven_v4 and both parameters were checked on ElevenLabs' documentation this pass; the tool could not be run here (no network, no key), and a dry run printed the right requests.
- Tags in the stories: a story's new `audio` field is its words with Eleven v4 tags added. The seventeen stories read so far carry them (the twelve of Very first steps and the five of Listen and point): sound effects used sparingly where the story already names the sound (a ball rolling on grass, a hen clucking, a big splash, a cow mooing in the distance, footsteps crunching in snow), feelings and delivery where a line turns ([puzzled], [whispers], [sighs], [delighted]), and [slowly, warmly] on each story's closing lesson line. The stories test proves that `audio` is `words` plus tags and nothing else, that no story shows a tag, and that every story fits one Eleven v4 request.
- Listen and point (pre-K 3), all five modules read by hand. A and B now says B is tall with two round bumps on one side (it said round on one side), and asks about alligator instead of arm, since arm does not start with the short a the other words teach. The green apple and Where is the dog? lose their colons, and in Where is the dog? Leo's sister now asks for the hat before he taps it. Circle and square, Which one is different? and Animal sounds read well and stay as they were.
- The tour's words (Mikey: edit the tour text too). Card 2 says each module comes back several times over several days and keeps returning in short memory checks after mastery; card 3's comma sits in the right place; card 4's run-on sentence is two sentences, and the questions shift toward emotional resilience and treat failure as a normal, useful part of learning; card 8 says lets you know (it said let's). The sample pages read well.
- Honest costs: ElevenLabs bills characters, tags included. The whole ledger is about 1.18 million characters; pre-K to grade 2 is about 230,000, a sensible first slice.
- Checks: rules 1,923, coverage 375, spelling 77, stories 6,379 (four new audio checks), ledger 6, story pictures 2, old backups 11, untaught 0 of 615; check.sh ALL CHECKS PASSED in one run (browser click-through 307, license 7, drag 7, educator 12, the tour walk 118 with the new card words, back 21, dark 55, standalone page 8).

The copy, which waited a moment before tapping, passed all 307. Two fixes: the app now stops those second asks the moment a person scrolls, taps or types, so a quick person keeps their place; and the test waits 400 milliseconds before the two Early years taps, since a test's scroll is not a person's input. The content changes were not the cause. The next full run passed the click-through but the drag test then failed the same way (its Let's Play tap came right after the overview opened and missed); it passed when run again, so it gets the same 400 millisecond wait after the overview opens.

Listen and tap: its questions asked for the moon and the flower, which the lesson never named; all six words are now taught with what each thing is or does, and its example, a bird captioned The sun, is now the sun. Animal sounds: many animals have a sound of their own, so a sound tells you which animal is near even when you cannot see it, said that way because a sound does not belong to one animal only (dogs growl too). Where is the dog? and Who said that? now close on the lesson's truth rather than an instruction.
- Explanations give the reason: a square has four corners, a circle is round with no corners, B has two round bumps, the moon is often seen at night.
- Citations: ELOF P-LIT 3 (identifies letters of the alphabet and produces correct sounds associated with letters), read on headstart.gov, fits A and B; the shapes, sorting and listening citations stand.
- Pre-K 3 is finished at the full standard: all seventeen modules, eighteen items with Scarcity.

## 2026-10-04 (pass JS): pre-K 4 First steps part two at the full standard, so First steps is finished

- Mikey said continue, not committed, so the commit message carries JN, JO, JP, JQ, JR and JS.
- Nine lessons read against the nine parts, and most had the gap the earlier passes found, questions about something the spoken lesson never showed. First strokes asked for waves and zigzags and showed only down, across and round; both are now drawn and described (a wave is smooth like water, a zigzag has sharp points like a lightning bolt). Draw the shapes asked to trace a star and a heart and showed neither; both are traced now, and every shape ends back at the dot. Patterns asked whether a row repeats and which piece is missing, and showed neither a row that does not repeat nor a gap; both are shown, with the way to find a gap (say the pattern and listen for it). Helpers all around asked who uses a hose, a stethoscope, a whiteboard and a mail truck, and never said; each helper now comes with that tool. One, two, three now says aloud that the last number tells how many, and asks Tap the group of 3 rather than Tap 3. Bigger and smaller teaches that size words compare two things (a cat is bigger than a mouse but smaller than a horse). Big, bigger, biggest says the middle one is bigger and smaller at once. Connect the dots says why the order matters (skip a dot and the picture comes out wrong). Taking turns tells turns from sharing and splits its old one-line list of rules into lines a child can follow.
- Three examples showed the wrong picture: Taking turns showed three dots for two children, Big, bigger, biggest a square captioned The biggest circle, and Helpers all around a flower captioned A firefighter puts out the fire. Each now shows what it says, and each is its own picture, as the rules ask.
- Explanations give the reason: That circle takes up the most room, so it is the biggest; Two dots are more than one dot, and the bigger group has more; Listening is the good choice, because it shows you care what they say (each good choice now carries its own reason).
- Citations: Bigger and smaller cited ELOF P-MATH 10 (positions in space) and now cites P-MATH 8; One, two, three adds P-MATH 3; Draw the shapes cited V.D.1 as names common shapes, but V.D.1 in pre-K 4 is the measurement guideline (compares heights or lengths), so that clause gives way to ELOF P-MATH 9 (identifies, describes, compares, and composes shapes), read on headstart.gov.

## 2026-10-04 (pass JV): kindergarten Letters finished at the full standard, letters drawn from the top, and a clean way to take a delivery

- Mikey said everything was committed, so the commit message starts fresh at JV. He sent two photos of his repository folder full of numbered copies (.gitignore 2 to 5, build 2.sh to 5, index 2.html and 3, sw 2.js to 5 and more) and asked to condense everything so he could delete his files and copy fresh ones. The delivered zip has none (116 files, none numbered); the copies are made on his Mac after download. He always chooses Replace, so Finder's Keep Both is not the cause. His Finder shows iCloud Drive status icons on the folder and on .git, the counts differ file by file and no folder was copied, which fits iCloud Drive's conflict copies, made when a file changes while it is still syncing. GitHub Desktop then lists the copies as new files, and a commit carries them to GitHub. CHANGES.md and screenshots/ in his folder are leftovers of the changes-only zip, which is retired (CLAUDE.md and HANDOFF no longer describe it).
- tools/update-from-zip.sh makes his folder match a delivered zip. It copies every file in fresh and removes every file the zip lacks, and it never touches .git, .gitattributes, ignored files, Claude Code's .claude/settings.local.json, or his own pictures and sounds in art/ and audio/. It refuses while anything is uncommitted, so whatever it removes can come back from git, and it warns about numbered copies inside .git and about folders iCloud may sync. Tested on a copy of his folder as the photos show it (41 copies and both leftovers removed, everything of his kept); tests/update-from-zip.test.mjs (24 checks, including that no project file has a space in its name) runs in check.sh. Advised: keep the repository where iCloud never syncs (a fresh clone into a Developer folder in the home folder), since iCloud can also copy and evict files inside .git; docs/RUNBOOK.md has the steps.
- Nine lessons read against the nine parts, and kindergarten Letters is finished. Sounding out: its script said "C, a, t", which a voice reads as the letter names see, ay and tee, and blending names never makes cat, the very mistake its own story warns about. Its questions did the same: the setup "h, e, n." and the explanation "The middle sound is e", read as ee. Sounds are now written for a voice, as the sound (cuh, sss) or by the word a vowel starts (the sound at the start of apple), never as a bare letter. The five short vowels are taught with anchor words (apple, egg, igloo, octopus, up), and the consonant sounds its words use are listed, since g, h, j, l, r and w had never been taught. The questions show the letters without naming them, since sounding them out is the child's job, and ask which letter makes the first, middle or last sound; explanations give the sounds in order. In the story the brother says the sounds, not the names, and the second door (D, O and G) is sounded out the same way; its picture S1567 showed the first door's cat and now shows the second door, with no dog behind it.
- Rhymes shows a pair that does not rhyme (cat and dog), which half its questions ask, and its example, which showed dog and log captioned cat and hat, shows cat and hat. Every explanation names the shared ending (both end with at), the same-ending question never asks "Up ends with up", and in the story a bat flies into the rhyme. Clapping syllables names syllables, teaches comparing claps (its questions ask which word has more and which has the same number) and the chin trick (the chin drops once for each beat); explanations are whole sentences (Monkey has two claps, mon and key) in place of fragments (Mon key. 2 claps.), and the setup says "Say the word monkey slowly", not "Say it slowly, monkey". Which way we read teaches with words, since every question is a line of words, along with the spaces between them (a question counts them), the return sweep, and that reading the other way scrambles the words; in the story, reading backward now reverses the words (dig can cat the) rather than spelling tac and gid. What a word means names every shape, solid and number word its questions use (it named two of fourteen); its example showed a triangle captioned circle; explanations give the reason a picture fits (It has three sides and three corners, so this is the triangle); the story's girl is Nia, since Rosa already leads this course's rhymes story.
- The four tracing lessons asked for letters they never showed: Tracing letters showed L and asked seventeen capitals, Tracing small letters showed l and o and asked nine, More letters to trace showed Z and K and asked fifteen. Each now traces every letter it asks, one family a lesson: lines and circles (L, T, I, H, E, F, O, C), the nine small letters, zigzags and crosses (Z, W, M, X, Y, K), and slants (V, A, N). Tracing letters and More letters to trace now ask fewer letters each. That bends the rule that questions are never reduced, for the learner: seventeen letters in one spoken lesson is too much for a five-year-old, and asking letters a lesson never shows is the gap the full standard closes. Mikey was told and said continue. Every letter is still asked in the course, and each round's review question revisits earlier lessons. Explanations describe the strokes (H is two lines down, then a bridge across the middle). The small letters lesson said small letters sit low while asking for l and t; now most sit low and a few reach up tall.
- Stroke order: N, M and the small n, h, m and r were drawn from the bottom up, while the lessons said down (Trace V, A and N said "Down, slant down, then up" over an N that started at the bottom), and TEKS K.2.E asks for appropriate directionality. All six now start at the top, as kindergarten handwriting is taught, and grade 1's lessons for n, h, m and r, which already said down, now match their pictures. A was described as slant up, while it is drawn as two slants down from its point; the lesson and the story now say so. In the tracing letters story, T goes across first, as the app draws it.
- Citations read in 19 TAC 110.2 (the Texas Administrative Code text on Justia, current through June 27, 2025): syllables add K.2.A.vi (segmenting multisyllabic words into syllables); sounding out adds K.2.A.viii (blending spoken phonemes to form one-syllable words) and CCSS RF.K.2.D; which way we read quotes K.2.D.ii exactly (reading moves from top to bottom and left to right with return sweep) and adds K.2.D.iii (words separated by spaces); the tracing lessons quote K.2.E with "using appropriate directionality"; word meanings quotes K.3.B exactly and adds K.3.C (words that name categories such as shapes). The plan has the new rows.
- Open: K.2.E asks for all uppercase and lowercase letters, and the app traces seventeen capitals and fifteen small letters; B, D, G, J, P, Q, R, S and U and the small a, b, d, e, f, g, j, p, q, s and y need stroke drawings, a later pass. Sound spellings such as lll, rrr and fff should get entries in an ElevenLabs pronunciation dictionary when the audio is made, so a voice holds the sound rather than spelling it.
- Three more from Mikey's message the same day. The standards map had two rows of toggles, by grade or by subject and core or elective; he asked for one, so the map is grouped by grade only and the core or elective toggle stays. His screenshot showed a saved note in the dark theme with its date and its Edit and Remove links nearly invisible. The note card stays light green in the dark theme, and those three kept the dark theme's pale colors; they now take dark ink on the light card, about five to one. The dark theme test never saved a note, which is why it missed this; it now saves one before it measures the report.
- Pictures beside comparisons. Mikey: the lesson that says a slice of pizza is almost a triangle would teach better with a slice beside the triangle. The pre-K 3 Triangles lesson already had painting P15 logged for that line (pass JP), but until a painting arrives a line shows its own drawn picture, so he saw only the triangle. The line now shows a drawn pizza slice beside the triangle (a new pizza drawing, its three corners lined up with the triangle's), and the painting replaces both when it comes. To find more places like it, tools/picture-candidates.mjs lists every early-years lesson line that compares an idea to a real thing without a picture of its own (docs/PICTURE-CANDIDATES.md, 41 lines today), and the full standard's picture part reads that list for every module it reviews (docs/FABLE-REVIEW.md, CLAUDE.md).
- Mikey solved the copies on his side: a new repository in a local folder, with iCloud's Desktop syncing turned off. He takes each zip in with Replace as before; tools/update-from-zip.sh is there when he wants the folder to match a zip exactly.
